1    Preface to the I3C Basic Specification

2    1. What is MIPI I3C Basic?
3    MIPI I3C Basic is a feature-reduced, lower-complexity version of the powerful, flexible, and efficient MIPI
4    I3C interface [MIPI02], suitable for a broad range of device interconnect applications including sensor and
5    memory interfacing.

6    2. Motivation
 7   MIPI Alliance considers I3C technology to have significant advantages over the widely adopted legacy
 8   interfaces currently used for similar applications, and wishes to see I3C technology broadly deployed in the
 9   industry. Being aware that some advanced I3C v1.x features are not needed for many common applications,
10   and that some potential users might regard MIPI Alliance membership as a barrier, the MIPI Board of
11   Directors decided to make the I3C Basic Specification freely available without requiring a MIPI Alliance
12   membership, and to facilitate a royalty free licensing environment for all implementers, as described further
13   below.

14   3. IPR Status
15   MIPI Alliance’s regular intellectual property rights-related terms apply only by and among members. To
16   address this issue, MIPI Alliance created a set of supplemental patent licensing terms for the current and
17   future versions of the I3C Basic Specification, attached to this document as Annex E. MIPI required that all
18   members participating in the development of the I3C Basic Specification agree to these additional terms.
19   These terms include an obligation to license applicable patent claims to both member and non-member
20   implementers, for uses both in mobile devices and otherwise, on “RAND-Z” terms: that is, under royalty free
21   (the Z references zero royalty) and otherwise reasonable and non-discriminatory terms.
22   As described in Annex E, any implementer that wishes to benefit from the RAND-Z obligation must also
23   commit to license other implementers under the same RAND-Z terms. This reciprocity requirement is
24   intended to expand royalty free license obligations through the broader I3C Basic ecosystem.
25   The MIPI member companies that joined the I3C Basic Specification development effort and manifest
26   agreement to the terms in Annex E are listed in Annex F. Annex F also notes if and when any party
27   terminates their agreement to these terms (subject to certain ongoing license obligations, as described in the
28   terms).
29   The I3C Basic IPR terms are intended to create a robust royalty free environment for implementers. However,
30   no set of IPR terms can comprehensively address all potential risks. The terms apply only to those parties
31   that agree to them, and the scope of application is limited to what is described in the terms. Implementers
32   must ultimately make their own risk assessment, and the disclaimers described elsewhere in this document
33   remain in full force and effect.

34   4. Relationship to MIPI I3C v1.x Specifications
35   The MIPI I3C Basic v1.0 Specification is a subset of the MIPI I3C v1.0 Specification, but also incorporates
36   certain elements of the draft MIPI I3C v1.1 Specification. In the I3C Basic Specification, the terms “I3C,”
37   “I3C Device,” and “I3C Bus” should be interpreted as referring to both I3C v1.x and I3C Basic v1.x.

38   4.1 I3C v1.0 Functions Not Included in I3C Basic
39   The following functions of the I3C v1.0 Specification have been removed from the I3C Basic v1.0
40   Specification. Companies interested in implementing these functions should join MIPI Alliance to enjoy
41   member IPR licensing.
42    • Timing Control (Section 5.1.8)
43    • HDR Double Data Rate Mode (HDR-DDR) (Section 5.2.2)
44    • HDR Ternary Modes (HDR-TSP and HDR-TSL) (Section 5.2.3)

45   4.2 I3C v1.1 Functions Included in I3C Basic
46   The following I3C Basic functions do not appear in MIPI I3C v1.0, but are expected in the forthcoming MIPI
47   I3C v1.1 Specification.
48     • Direct Read/Write CCC Capability (Section 5.1.9.1, Category 4)
49     • Get Max Data Speed (GETMXDS) Refinement (Section 5.1.9.3.18)
50     • SETAASA CCC (Section 5.1.9.3.22)
51     • Low Voltage / High Capacitive Load IO (Section 6, Table 55)

52   5. How I3C Basic Devices Work with I3C v1.x Devices
53   MIPI I3C Basic Devices, whether Main Master, Secondary Master, or Slave, are intended to be interoperable
54   with I3C v1.x Devices for the set of optional features that are mutually supported by the connected Devices.
55   It is easiest to view I3C Basic as a subset of I3C, with a specific set of additional, optional features that are
56   only offered to MIPI Alliance Members.

     1      Introduction
57   The proliferation of sensors in mobile wireless and mobile-influenced products has created significant design
58   challenges. Because there are no consistent methods for interfacing physical sensors, Device and platform
59   designers are faced with digital interface fragmentation that includes I2C, SPI, and UART among others.
60   In addition to the main interface other signals may be needed, such as dedicated interrupts, chip select signals,
61   and enable and sleep signals. This increases the required number of Host GPIOs, and that in turn drives up
62   system cost with more Host package pins and more PCB layers.
63   As time passes and the number of sensors increases, this situation is becoming increasingly difficult to
64   support and manage.
65   The MIPI I3C interface has been developed to ease sensor system design architectures in mobile wireless
66   products by providing a fast, low cost, low power, two-wire digital interface for sensors.

     1.1     Scope
71   The following topics are in scope for this Specification:
72    • I3C interface protocols and commands leveraged for I3C Basic
73    • Electrical specifications, such as timing and voltage levels
74     • Support for specific classes of sensors and other Devices
75   The following topics are out of scope for this Specification:
76    • Mechanical, system, and implementation details within an I3C Device
77    • ESD (Electrostatic Discharge) structures
78    • System power management
79    • Use case specific data or format definitions besides Bus management command codes

     1.2     I3C Purpose
80   The I3C interface is intended to improve upon the features of the I2C interface [NXP01], preserving backward
81   compatibility. This Specification defines a standard Multi-Drop interface between Host processors and
82   peripheral sensors.
83   Implementing the I3C Specification greatly increases the flexibility mobile terminal system designers have
84   to support an ever-expanding sensor subsystem as efficiently and at as low a cost as possible.

      1.3        I3C Key Features
85    Two main concerns are paramount for the I3C interface: The use of as little energy as possible in transporting
86    data and control, while reducing the number of physical pins used by the interface.
87    Therefore, the I3C interface features:
88     • Two wire serial interface up to 12.5 MHz using Push-Pull
89     • Legacy I2C Device co-existence on the same Bus (with some limitations)
90     • Dynamic Addressing while supporting Static Addressing for Legacy I2C Devices
91     • Legacy I2C messaging
92     • I2C-like Single Data Rate messaging (SDR)
93     • NOT SUPPORTED IN I3C BASIC: Optional High Data Rate messaging Modes (HDR)
94     • Multi-Drop capability
95     • Multi-Master capability
96     • In-Band Interrupt support
97     • Hot-Join support
98     • NOT SUPPORTED IN I3C BASIC: Synchronous Timing Support and Asynchronous Time
99       Stamping
100   The I3C interface provides major efficiencies in Bus power while providing greater than 10x speed
101   improvements over I2C. Figure 2 and Figure 3 show different Bus Mode options that provide tradeoffs for
102   performance/power vs. target Device complexity.

               The bar chart on the left shows energy consumption for a given amount of data for the different I3C modes,
               compared to I2C. The bar chart on the right shows the same comparison for data throughput.
               Both charts show a significant advantage for I3C.

      2     Terminology

      2.1      Use of Special Terms
111   The MIPI Alliance has adopted Section 13.1 of the IEEE Standards Style Manual, which dictates use of the
112   words “shall”, “should”, “may”, and “can” in the development of documentation, as follows:
113            The word shall is used to indicate mandatory requirements strictly to be followed in order
114            to conform to the Specification and from which no deviation is permitted (shall equals is
115            required to).
116            The use of the word must is deprecated and shall not be used when stating mandatory
117            requirements; must is used only to describe unavoidable situations.
118            The use of the word will is deprecated and shall not be used when stating mandatory
119            requirements; will is only used in statements of fact.
120            The word should is used to indicate that among several possibilities one is recommended
121            as particularly suitable, without mentioning or excluding others; or that a certain course of
122            action is preferred but not necessarily required; or that (in the negative form) a certain
123            course of action is deprecated but not prohibited (should equals is recommended that).
124            The word may is used to indicate a course of action permissible within the limits of the
125            Specification (may equals is permitted to).
126            The word can is used for statements of possibility and capability, whether material,
127            physical, or causal (can equals is able to).
128   All sections are normative, unless they are explicitly indicated to be informative.
129   All quoted voltage and frequency values in the informative sections represent their typical values.

      2.2      Definitions
130   ACK: Short for “acknowledge”. See also NACK.
131   Address Arbitration: Process for determining arbitrated Addresses to resolve contention.
132   Address: A set of bits designating a Device or the location of a register.
133   Arbitrable: Subject to decision by Arbitration.
134   Arbitration: If two Devices start transmission at the same time, then Arbitration is required to determine
135   Bus control. Arbitration could also be required during a Slave transmission if a Master addresses multiple
136   Slaves.
137   Bridge Device: Device on the I3C Bus that allows conversion from the native I3C Bus protocol to another
138   protocol (such as SPI, UART etc.).
139   Broadcast: A command intended for multiple Slave Devices, using the Broadcast Address 7'h7E.
140   Bus: The physical and logical implementation of the SCL and SDA lines.
141   Bus Available Condition: State on the I3C Bus where both the SCL line and the SDA line are High for at
142   least tAVAL (see Table 58), and a Device is able to initiate a transaction on the Bus.
143   Bus Free Condition: State on the I3C Bus after a STOP and before a START for at least tCAS (see Table 58).
144   Bus Idle Condition: An extended duration of the Bus Free Condition that indicates that Devices may attempt
145   to Hot-Join the I3C Bus.
146   Bus Turnaround: When a transmitting Device sends a command, and then the receiving Device takes over
147   the I3C Bus in order to respond.
148   Characteristics: Quantification of a Device’s available features and capabilities.

                                      Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                      7
                                                 All rights reserved.
                                                    Confidential
      Specification for I3C Basic                                                                      Version 1.0
                                                                                                      19-Jul-2018

149   Clock to Data Turnaround Time: The time duration between reception of an SCL edge and the start of
150   driving an SDA change. See tSCO in Table 59.
151   CRC5: Cyclic Redundancy Check with fifth-order polynomial length.
152   Current Master: The I3C Device that presently has Master control of the I3C Bus.
153   Device: A Master or Slave.
154   Device ID: Defines a Device’s characteristic or function within a sensor system.
155   Dynamic Address: A Device Address that is assigned or allocated during initialization of the Bus. Usually
156   occurs after power up.
157   Failsafe: An I3C Device Bus pad is considered Failsafe if its leakage does not increase when it is unpowered.
158   A pad may be unpowered because the Device is unpowered, because its IO rail is unpowered or clamped, or
159   both. Failsafe only matters for some Hot-Join Devices.
160   Frame: A Frame begins with a START, followed by the Address of the targeted Slave(s), Data, and finally a
161   STOP.
162   High: Defines a signal level that is a logical “1”.
163   High Data Rate (HDR): High Data Rate Modes that achieve higher speed by transferring data on both clock
164   edges.
165   High-Keeper: A weak Pull-Up type Device used when SDA, and sometimes SCL, is in High-Z with respect
166   to all Devices.
167   Host: Hardware and software that provides the core functionality of a mobile device.
168   Hot-Join: Slaves that join the Bus after it is already started, whether because they were not powered
169   previously or because they were physically inserted into the Bus; the Hot-Join mechanism allows the Slave
170   to notify the Master that it is ready to get a Dynamic Address.
171   In-Band Interrupt (IBI): A method whereby a Slave Device emits its Address into the arbitrated Address
172   header on the I3C Bus to notify the Master of an interrupt.
173   I2C Device: A Master or Slave that meets the requirements of the I2C Specification [NXP01].
174   I3C Basic Device: A Master or Slave that meets the requirements of the I3C Basic Specification (this
175   document).
176   I3C v1.x Device: A Master or Slave that meets the requirements of the MIPI I3C Specification, version 1.0
177   [MIPI02] or any subsequently developed MIPI I3C Specification version 1.1, 1.2, etc.
178   I3C Slave: See Slave.
179   Legacy I2C: I3C maintains the industry standard architecture of I2C and supports existing I2C Slave Devices.
180   I3C does not support I2C Bus Masters.
181   Low: Defines a signal level that is a logical “0”.
182   Main Master: Master that has overall control of the I3C Bus. Including control and hand off to Secondary
183   Masters.
184   Master: A reference to the I3C Bus Device that is controlling the Bus.
185   Mastership: Control of the I3C Bus, in a Master role.
186   Message: A packetized communication between Devices.
187   Minimal Bus: An I3C Bus with one Master Device (potentially with reduced functionality), and one active
188   Slave Device with a fixed and reserved Slave Address value of 7’h01. Additional Read-only Slave Devices
189   may optionally also be present in a Minimal Bus, but there can be no additional Read-Write Slave Devices.
190   MIPI Manufacturer ID: A two byte/16 bit unique identifier for a vendor of a MIPI compliant Device
191   [MIPI01].


      8                                Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
      Version 1.0                                                                       Specification for I3C Basic
      19-Jul-2018

192   Mixed Fast Bus: I3C Bus topology with both I2C and I3C Devices present on the I3C Bus, where the I2C
193   Devices have a true I2C 50 ns Spike Filter on the SCL line.
194   Mixed Slow/Limited Bus: I3C Bus topology with both I2C and I3C Devices present on the I3C Bus, where
195   the I2C Devices do not have a true I2C 50 ns Spike Filter on the SCL line.
196   Mode: Distinguishes different data transfer methods used in I3C including Legacy I2C Mode, Single Data
197   Rate Mode (SDR), and High Data Rate Mode (HDR).
198   Multi-Drop: A Bus that communicates through a process of Arbitration to determine which Device sends
199   information at any point. The other Devices listen for data they are intended to receive.
200   Multi-Master: Multiple Bus Masters present on the Bus. Used when multiple nodes on the Bus need to
201   initiate a transfer.
202   NACK: Short for “not acknowledge”, which means No ACK was asserted. See also ACK.
203   Offline Capable: An Offline Capable Device is able to disconnect from the physical I3C Bus and/or is able
204   to ignore I3C traffic on the I3C Bus. A Device’s Offline capability is one of the capabilities reflected in its
205   Bus Characterization Register.
206   Open Drain: High-Z with an active Pull-Down. Typically used in conjunction with a passive Pull-Up.
207   Park: Logic level High set by the Master (or Slave on Read) before turning around the Bus to allow the other
208   to drive to Logic level Low or not (will be held High by weak Pull-Up).
209   Pull-Down: Active mechanism used to pull the Bus to a logical Low state.
210   Pull-Up: Mechanism used to pull the Bus to a logical High state. The mechanism may be either active or
211   passive.
212   Pure Bus: A Bus topology with only I3C Devices present. No I2C Devices are permitted on a Pure Bus.
213   Push-Pull: Active Pull-Down and active Pull-Up on output driver.
214   Repeated START: Two or more instances of a START in a row without an intervening STOP. A Repeated
215   START is used in circumstances where the Master wishes to continue communicating on the I3C Bus without
216   having to first generate a STOP. In this Specification, a Repeated START is abbreviated as “Sr”. This is
217   equivalent to repeated START in I2C [NXP01].
218   SDR-Only: An SDR-Only Device supports only SDR Mode, i.e. does not support HDR Mode.
219   Secondary Master: A Secondary Master controls the I3C Bus only after receiving permission from the Main
220   Master. Control of the Bus is temporary, and always ends with passing control back to the Main Master.
221   Single Data Rate (SDR): Single Data Rate transfers data on only one edge of the clock.
222   Slave: A Slave Device can only respond to either Common or individual commands from a Master. A Slave
223   Device cannot generate a clock.
224   Spike Filter: A filter that removes SCL (and SDA) spikes shorter than 50 ns in duration. See Input Filter (tSP
225   parameter) in the I2C Specification [NXP01]. Also known as a glitch filter.
226   Stall: The act of the I3C Master holding the SCL line Low under specific transitory conditions.
227   START: START is the I3C Bus condition of a High to Low transition on the SDA line while the SCL line
228   remains High. In this Specification, a START is abbreviated as “S”.
229   START Request: A method for a Slave to force the Master to issue a START on an idled I3C Bus.
230   Static Address: A Device Address that is fixed and cannot be changed.
231   STOP: STOP is the I3C Bus condition of a Low to High transition on the SDA line while the SCL line
232   remains High. In this Specification, a STOP is abbreviated as “P”.
233   T-Bit: Transition bit, an alternative to the ACK/NACK mechanism.
234   Word: Transmission containing 16 payload bits and two parity bits.

235   Word Types: Four Word Types are used: Command Word, User Data Word, CRC Word, and Reserved Word.

      4     Technical Overview (Informative)
286   This Section generally describes the I3C Bus, the I3C interface, and I3C Master and Slave Devices.
287   I3C is a two-wire bidirectional serial Bus, optimized for multiple sensor Slave Devices and controlled by
288   only one I3C Master Device at a time. I3C is backward compatible with many Legacy I2C Devices, but I3C
289   Devices also support significantly higher speeds, new communication Modes, and new Device roles,
290   including an ability to change Device Roles over time (i.e., the initial Master can cooperatively pass the
291   Master role to another I3C Device on the Bus, if the second I3C Device supports that feature).
292   I3C Basic includes:
293     • Support for many Legacy I2C Slave Devices and messages
294     • I3C Single Data Rate (SDR) Mode: New I3C enhanced version of the I2C protocol supporting
295       private messages, and adding two kinds of standard built-in messages:
296       • Broadcast messages, which are sent to all I3C Slaves on the Bus
297       • Direct messages, which are addressed to specific Slaves

      4.1     I3C Fundamental Principles
298   I3C supports several communication formats, all sharing a two-wire interface.
299   The two wires are designated SDA and SCL:
300    • SDA (Serial Data) is a bidirectional data pin
301    • SCL (Serial Clock) is a clock pin
302   An I3C Bus supports the mixing of various Message types:
303   1. I2C-like SDR Messages, with SCL clock speeds up to 12.5MHz
304   2. Broadcast and Direct Common Command Code (CCC) Messages that allow the Master to
305       communicate to all or one of the Slaves on the I3C Bus, respectively
306   3. NOT SUPPORTED IN I3C BASIC: HDR Mode Messages, which achieve higher data rates per
307       equivalent clock cycle
308   4. I2C Messages to Legacy I2C Slaves
309   5. Slave-initiated START Request to the Master, for example to send an In-Band Interrupt or to
310       request the Master role
311   An example traffic pattern on the I3C Bus is shown in Figure 4.



                                       I2C-Like:                          Slave
                                      One or More
                                                                        Interrupt
                                     MS W and R

314   Figure 5 illustrates how I3C communication is initiated:
315    • All I3C communication occurs within a Frame. The Frame begins with a START, followed by one
316      or more transfers, and a STOP.
317    • For the HDR Modes that are not supported in I3C Basic, but are tolerated by I3C Basic Devices:
318       • First the dedicated Broadcast I3C Address (7’h7E) is issued to all Slaves on the I3C Bus.
319       • Then one of the EnterHDR CCCs is issued, indicating that the Master is entering an HDR
320          Mode. Each HDR Mode has its own EnterHDR CCC.
321       • This is followed by one or more HDR transfers.
322       • HDR Mode is ended by using the HDR Exit Pattern protocol.

                                                                         All rights reserved.
                                                                            Confidential
      Version 1.0                                                                       Specification for I3C Basic
      19-Jul-2018

325   I3C is based on a Frame encapsulation approach. A Frame includes a Data Payload.
326   The I3C Basic Frame always includes at least the START, the Header, the Data, and the STOP.
327   The Header following a START allows for Bus Arbitration. The Master uses the Header to address Slave
328   Device(s). Slave Devices(s) may use the Header Arbitration for multiple purposes: for In-Band Interrupt, for
329   Hot-Join, and for Secondary Master functionality.
330   I3C allows only one Master to have control of the I3C Bus at a time. Mechanisms for handoff of the Master
331   role from one Device to another Device are provided.

      4.2      I3C Master and Slave Devices
332   A given I3C Bus always has one Master and one or more Slaves. This Section generally describes I3C Master
333   Devices and I3C Slave Devices.
334   A given I3C Device can be designed to function either solely as an I3C Master, solely as an I3C Slave, or
335   with both I3C Master and I3C Slave capabilities.
336   An I3C Device with both I3C Master and I3C Slave capabilities cannot function as both Master and Slave at
337   the same time, instead it must be configured either as an I3C Slave Device or as an I3C Master Device. Such
338   an I3C Device can be initially configured (initialized) on an I3C Bus either as the Master of that I3C Bus, or
339   as a Slave on that I3C Bus. However for that I3C Bus to function properly, only one of the multiple I3C
340   Devices on the Bus can be initially configured (initialized) as an I3C Master Device. That I3C Device will
341   have the ‘Main Master’ Device Role, and will be the first I3C Device on the Bus to serve as Current Master;
342   all other I3C Devices and Legacy I2C Devices on the I3C Bus will be initially configured (initialized) as
343   Slaves.
344   I3C introduces the concept of Current Master, defined as the I3C Master Device on the I3C Bus that is
345   functioning as Master (i.e., the one that is controlling the Bus) at the present time. Only one I3C Device on
346   an I3C Bus can serve as Current Master at a time. However after initial Bus configuration the Current Master
347   function can be cooperatively passed from the Current Master to any other I3C Device on the Bus with I3C
348   Master Device capability, using provided I3C commands (CCCs).
349   I3C defines several Master and Slave Device Roles (see Table 2 and Table 3) to reflect the functional
350   capabilities of a given I3C Master or Slave Device. A given I3C Device must support at least one Device
351   Role, and can be designed to support multiple Device Roles. Every I3C Device exposes the Device Roles it
352   supports via its Bus Characteristics Register (BCR, see Section 5.1.1.2.1).
353                                Table 2 Roles for I3C Compatible Devices
         Device
                               Device Role                                     Description
          Type
       I3C            I3C Main Master                   Initially configures I3C Bus, has HDR support
       Master1
                      SDR-Only Main Master              Initially configures I3C Bus, no HDR support
                      (I3C Basic)
                      I3C Secondary Master              Can Master but currently functioning as Slave
                      SDR-Only Secondary Master         Can Master but currently functioning as Slave, no HDR
                      (I3C Basic)                       support
       I3C Slave2     I3C Slave                         Ordinary I3C Slave, no Master capability
                      I2C Slave                         No I3C Master or I3C Slave capabilities
       Note:
          1)   Applies to Master-only Devices. In a Multi-Master context a Master Device may also implement
               functionality to join the Bus acting in a Slave role.
          2)   Applies to Slave-only Devices. In a Multi-Master context a Slave Device may also implement
               functionality to join the Bus acting in a Master role.


                                      Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                  15
                                                 All rights reserved.

                                                                                                                       19-Jul-2018

      4.2.1      I3C Master Device
354   An I3C Bus requires there to be exactly one I3C Device at a time functioning as an I3C Master Device. In
355   I3C terms, this I3C Master Device is the Current Master at that time. In typical applications, the Current
356   Master is the I3C Device on the Bus that sends the majority of the I3C Commands (CCC), addressing either
357   all Slaves (Broadcast CCCs) or specific individual Slaves (Directed CCCs). The Current Master is also the
358   only Device on the I3C Bus allowed to send I2C Messages.
359   In addition to sending I3C Commands and I2C Messages, an I3C Master Device also:
360     • Generates the Bus clock
361     • Manages Pull-Up structures
362     • Manages the Dynamic Address Assignment procedure (including Hot-Join events) while acting as
363        the Main Master
364     • Manages START Requests from I3C Slave Devices on the Bus along with Address Arbitration
365        requests:
366        • Generate In-Band Interrupts
367        • For Hot-Join events
368        • To become Current Master
369     • Supports I2C Legacy Slave Devices
370     • Supports I3C SDR Mode
371   Figure 6 is a block diagram of a typical generic I3C Master Device.


                        Generic Logic Processor

      4.2.1.1        I3C Master Device Roles
374   All I3C Master Devices support one of the two Main Master Device Roles, and may also support one of the
375   two Secondary Master Device Roles.
376   Main Master Device Roles:
377    • Main Master: The I3C Master Device on the I3C Bus that initially configures the I3C Bus and
378      serves as the first Current Master. Only one I3C Device on a given I3C Bus can take the Main
379      Master role, i.e. the role cannot be passed on to any other I3C Device on the I3C Bus.
380    • SDR-Only Main Master: A Main Master that only supports I3C’s SDR Mode, i.e. does not
381      support any of the HDR Modes.
382   Secondary Master Device Roles:
383     • I3C Secondary Master: Any I3C Device on the I3C Bus, other than the Current Master, with I3C
384       Master Capability. There can be multiple Secondary Masters on an I3C Bus at the same time. By
385       definition, a Secondary Master functions as an I3C Slave Device until and unless it eventually
386       becomes Current Master.
387     • SDR-Only Secondary Master: A Secondary Master that only supports I3C’s SDR Mode, i.e.
388       does not support any of the HDR Modes.
389   See also Table 2 and Table 3.
390   Note:
391       Current Master is not formally defined as an I3C Device Role, and is not exposed in the I3C Device’s
392       Bus Characteristics Register (BCR, see Section 5.1.1.2.1).




                                      Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                  17
                                                 All rights reserved.
                                                    Confidential
      Specification for I3C Basic                                                                                    Version 1.0
                                                                                                                     19-Jul-2018

      4.2.2      I3C Slave Device
393   An I3C Bus supports up to 11 I3C Slave Devices, though the maximum number of Devices will depend on
394   trace length, capacitive load per Device, and the types of Devices (I2C vs. I3C) present on the Bus, because
395   these factors affect clock frequency requirements.
396   An I3C Slave Device listens to the I3C Bus for relevant I3C Commands (CCCs) sent by the Current Master,
397   and responds accordingly. This includes all Broadcast Commands (CCC), and any Directed Commands
398   (CCC) addressed specifically to that I3C Slave Device and supported by that I3C Slave Device.
399   In addition to responding to I3C Commands, an I3C Slave Device always supports I3C SDR Mode.
400   In addition, an I3C Slave Device can optionally:
401     • Request In-Band Interrupts
402     • Generate Hot-Join events
403     • Request to become Current Master, if the I3C Slave Device also has I3C Master Device capability
404   While functioning as a Slave, an I3C Slave Device functions in one of the Slave Device Roles detailed in
405   Section 4.2.2.1.
406   Figure 7 is a block diagram of a typical generic I3C Slave Device.

                         Generic Logic Processor

      4.2.2.1        I3C Slave Device Roles
409   All I3C Slave Devices support one of the two I3C Slave Device Roles:
410     • I3C Slave: An ordinary I3C Slave Device without Master capability.
411     • SDR-Only I3C Slave: An I3C Slave without Master capability that only supports I3C's SDR
412        Mode (i.e., does not support any of the HDR Modes).
413   Note:
414       An additional Slave Device Role is defined for I2C Slave, however this is not relevant for an I3C Slave
415       Device.
416   See also Table 2 and Table 3.

      5     I3C Protocol
417   This Section specifies the communication protocols for all defined I3C Modes:
418     • Single Data Rate (SDR) Mode: See Section 5.1
419     • NOT SUPPORTED IN I3C BASIC: High Data Rate (HDR) Modes: See Section 5.2
420       • HDR Ternary Symbol Pure-bus (HDR-TSP) Mode
421       • HDR Ternary Symbol Legacy-inclusive-bus (HDR-TSL) Mode
422       • HDR Double Data Rate (HDR-DDR) Mode
423   It is important to note that the I3C Bus is always initialized and configured in SDR Mode, never in any of
424   the HDR Modes. (The procedure for entering an HDR Mode from SDR Mode is detailed in Section 5.2.)
425   As a result, most of the essential basic I3C protocol specifications are found in Section 5.1, including:

426
                                              Subject                          Section
                             Bus Configuration                                   5.1.1
                             Bus Communication                                   5.1.2
                             Bus Free Condition                                 5.1.3.2
                             Bus Idle Condition                                 5.1.3.4
                             Bus Initialization and
                                                                                 5.1.4
                             Dynamic Address Assignment Mode
                             Hot-Join Mechanism                                  5.1.5
                             In-Band Interrupt                                   5.1.6
                             Secondary Master Functions                          5.1.7
                             Timing Control                                      5.1.8
                             Common Command Codes (CCC)                          5.1.9
                             Error Detection and Recovery Methods               5.1.10
                             High Data Rate (HDR) Modes                           5.2


      5.1      Single Data Rate (SDR) Mode
427   This Section specifies the communication protocols for Single Data Rate (SDR) Mode.
428   SDR Mode is the default Mode of the I3C Bus, and is primarily used for private messaging from the Current
429   Master Device to Slave Devices. SDR Mode is also used to enter other Modes, sub-Modes, and states (as
430   described in Section 5.1 and Section 5.2); and for built-in features such as Common Commands (CCCs), In-
431   Band Interrupts, and transition from I2C to I3C by assignment of a Dynamic Address.
432   I3C SDR Mode is significantly similar to the I2C protocol [NXP01] in terms of procedures and conditions,
433   and as a result I3C Devices and many Legacy I2C Slave Devices (but not I2C Master Devices) can coexist on
434   the same I3C Bus. However SDR Mode also includes numerous new features not present in I2C. For the
435   procedures and conditions that I3C shares with I2C, SDR Mode closely follows the definitions in the I2C
436   Specification. I2C traffic from an I3C Master to an I2C Slave will be properly ignored by all I3C Slaves,
437   because the I3C protocol is designed to allow I2C traffic. I3C traffic from an I3C Master to an I3C Slave will
438   not be seen by most Legacy I2C Slave Devices, because the I2C Spike Filter is opaque to I3C’s higher clock
439   speed.




                                      Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                   21
                                                 All rights reserved.
                                                    Confidential
      Specification for I3C Basic                                                                              Version 1.0
                                                                                                               19-Jul-2018

      5.1.1      Bus Configuration
440   The I3C Bus can be configured as the link among several clients, in a flexible and efficient manner. At the
441   system architecture level, eight roles are defined for I3C compatible Devices (see Table 2).
442   An example block diagram of I3C interconnections is shown in Figure 8. In this diagram the color blue
443   indicates Devices with a Master role, the color pink indicates Devices with an I3C Slave role, and the color
444   purple indicates Devices with an I2C Slave role. Note that I3C Secondary Master Devices are shaded from
445   blue to pink, illustrating their ability to function in both Master and Slave roles (at different times).



                                             I3C                              I3C                 I2C
                                          SECONDARY                          SLAVE               SLAVE
                                           MASTER




                                  SDA
                    I3C
                   MAIN
                  MASTER          SCL




                                             I3C                           I3C
                                                                                                  I2C
                                            SLAVE                      SECONDARY
                                                                                                 SLAVE
                                                                         MASTER




                         LEGEND

                                                    MASTER                           I3C SLAVE     I2C SLAVE
                               MASTER
                                                    (Slave of Main Master)

446
447                           Figure 8 I3C Bus with I2C Devices and I3C Devices

448   I3C compatible Devices may have diverse features, as appropriate for their function within the I3C Bus.
449   Depending on the I3C Bus’ system design, it may not be necessary for all features of a given Device to be
450   enabled for any particular Bus instantiation. However, the enabled features of every I3C compatible Device
451   shall be described in Characteristics Registers associated with the Device, as described in Section 5.1.1.2.
452   The I3C Main Master shall obtain the Characteristics of any Legacy I2C Devices on the I3C Bus before power
453   up (e.g. the fixed Address of each Legacy I2C Device present on the Bus).
454   At every start-up from a powered-down state, the Main Master shall assign a unique Dynamic Address to
455   every Device on the Bus, including itself. The Dynamic Address assignment procedure is described in
456   Section 5.1.4. Dynamic Addresses create a priority ranking of the Devices’ In-Band Interrupts. Any
457   Secondary Masters present on the I3C Bus shall be made aware of the Dynamic Address assignments and
458   Characteristic Registers associated with each I3C compatible Device on the Bus via Common Command
459   Codes as described in Section 5.1.9.

      5.1.1.2        I3C Characteristics Registers
475   I3C Characteristics Registers describe and define an I3C compatible Device’s capabilities and functions on
476   the I3C Bus, as the Device services a given system. Devices without I3C Characteristics Registers shall not
477   be connected to a common I3C Bus.
478   There are three Characteristics Register types:
479    • Bus Characteristics Register (BCR, see Section 5.1.1.2.1)
480    • Device Characteristics Register (DCR, see Section 5.1.1.2.2)
481    • Legacy Virtual Register (LVR, see Section 5.1.1.2.3)
482   Every I3C compatible Device shall have associated Characteristics Registers, depending on the Device type
483   as described below:
484     • Every I3C compliant Device (as defined in Table 3) shall have one Bus Characteristics Register,
485       and one Device Characteristics Register.
486     • Every Legacy I2C Device to be connected to an I3C Bus shall have one associated Legacy Virtual
487       Register. Since these are Legacy Devices, it is understood that this register will exist virtually, for
488       example as part of the Device’s driver.




                                     Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                25
                                                All rights reserved.
                                                   Confidential
      Specification for I3C Basic                                                                        Version 1.0
                                                                                                        19-Jul-2018

      5.1.1.2.1         Bus Characteristics Register (BCR)
489   Each I3C Device that is connected to the I3C Bus shall have an associated read-only Bus Characteristics
490   Register (BCR). This read-only register describes the I3C compliant Device’s role and capabilities for use in
491   Dynamic Address assignment and Common Command Codes. The bits within the BCR shall conform to the
492   Descriptions presented in in Table 6.
493                                 Table 6 Bus Characteristics Register (BCR)
           BIT                       Name                                        Description
                                                             2’b00 – I3C Slave
       BCR [7]        Device Role [1]
                                                             2’b01 – I3C Master1
                                                             2’b10 – Reserved for future definition by MIPI
       BCR [6]        Device Role [0]
                                                             2’b11 – Reserved for future definition by MIPI
       BCR [5]        MIPI Reserved                          0 – Default
                                                             0 – Not a Bridge Device
       BCR [4]        Bridge Identifier2
                                                             1 – Is a Bridge Device
                                                             0 – Device will always respond to I3C Bus commands
       BCR [3]        Offline Capable3                       1 – Device will not always respond to I3C Bus
                                                                commands
                                                             0 – No data byte follows the accepted IBI
       BCR [2]        IBI Payload                            1 – Mandatory one or more data bytes follow the
                                                                accepted IBI. Data byte continuation is indicated
                                                                by T-Bit, as described in Section 5.1.2.3.4
                                                             0 – Not Capable
       BCR [1]        IBI Request Capable
                                                             1 – Capable
                                                             0 – No Limitation
       BCR [0]        Max Data Speed Limitation4
                                                             1 – Limitation
       Note:
           1)    For an I3C Device acting as I3C Main Master, the BCR Device Role bits will contain the value
                 2’b01.
           2)    Bridge Devices are required to comply with the MIPI Specification for I3C v 1.0 [MIPI02].
           3)    Offline Capable Devices retain the Dynamic Address, and are specified in Section 2.2.
           4)    Master shall use the GETMXDS CCC to interrogate the Slave for specific limitation.

      5.1.1.2.2          Device Characteristics Register (DCR)
494   Each I3C Device that is connected to the I3C Bus shall have an associated read-only Device Characteristics
495   Register (DCR). This read-only register describes the I3C compliant Device type (e.g. accelerometer,
496   gyroscope, etc.) for use in Dynamic Address assignment and Common Command Codes. The bits within the
497   DCR shall conform to the Descriptions presented in Table 7.

      5.1.1.2.3          Legacy Virtual Register (LVR)
                     2

501   understood that this register will exist virtually, for example as part of the Device’s driver. When Legacy I2C
502   Devices are present on an I3C Bus, LVR data determines allowed Modes and maximum SCL clock frequency.
503   The bits within the LVR shall conform to the descriptions in Table 8.

      5.1.2      Bus Communication
509   The primary protocol and Mode of I3C is SDR (Single Data Rate) Mode. The SDR protocol is based on the
510   I2C standard protocol [NXP01], with a few notable variations:
511     • The I3C START and STOP (as shown in Figure 37 and Figure 39, respectively) are identical to
512       the I2C START and STOP in their signaling, but they may vary from I2C in their timing. Compare
513       Table 58 vs. Table 57.
514     • The I3C Address Header is identical to the I2C Address Header in bit form and in signaling, but it
515       may vary from I2C in its timing. See Section 5.1.2.2, Table 58, and Table 59.
516     • The Data 9-bit Words use the same bit count as I2C, but differ in the ninth bit, as explained in
517       Section 5.1.2.3.
518     • The I3C Data is normally sent using Push-Pull signaling, whereas I2C uses Open Drain signaling.
519       There are exceptions, including use in DAA (see Section 5.1.4.2) and an allowance for the Master
520       and Slave to agree to let the Slave to return Read operations using Open Drain. The use of Push-
521       Pull impacts transitions between Master and Slave. See Section 5.1.2.3 and its sub-sections.
522     • In I3C, the SCL line is only driven by the Master. Normally this drive is Push-Pull, but it can also
523       be Open Drain.
524   Because the bit count is the same for Address Header and Data Word, the I3C Slave only needs to know
525   whether a Message is an I3C Message (vs. is an I2C Message) if the Message is addressed to that Slave (either
526   directly or by Broadcast).
527   An I3C Message is defined as everything from the initial START (or Repeated START) to the next Repeated
528   START or STOP.
529   An I3C Message is an SDR Message if:
530    • The Address in the Address Header is 7’h7E (the I3C Broadcast Address). All I3C Slaves shall
531      match Address value 7’h7E. No I2C Slave will match Address 7’h7E, because that value is
532      reserved and unused in I2C.
533    • The Address in the Address Header matches the Slave’s Dynamic Address (as assigned by the I3C
534      Master per Section 5.1.4). All I3C Slaves shall match their own Dynamic Address. (It is permitted
535      to then NACK the Header if needed.)
536   All I3C Slaves shall ignore all Messages with Addresses other than 7’h7E or the I3C Master Assigned
537   Address, and await either the Repeated START or the STOP. I3C Slaves shall not transmit on the Bus in
538   response to a non-matching Address.
539   Note:
540        Legacy I2C Slaves will ignore any Message not addressed to them, and will await the next START or
541        STOP. Per Section 5.1.2.4, Legacy I2C Slaves may also not see some or all I3C Messages and
542        Modes due to the speed of SCL signaling.




      28                              Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                 All rights reserved.
                                                    Confidential

      19-Jul-2018

      5.1.2.1         Role of I3C Slave
545   The I3C Slave does not have to know whether it is on a Legacy I2C Bus or an I3C Bus. If it has a Legacy I2C
546   Static Address, then it may participate using that Address up until (and if) it is assigned a Dynamic Address.
547   Once assigned a Dynamic Address, unless asked to Reset, it shall only operate as an I3C Slave.
548   An I3C Capable Slave may act as an I2C Device before it gets its Dynamic Address (DA) assigned. However,
549   the Slave shall ACK the START with address 7’h7E. (The only exception would be if the Slave is choosing
550   to remain an I2C-only Device on a given bus or use, in which case it would leave its 50ns Spike Filter
551   enabled.)
552   Slaves that do recognize START and the 7’h7E address may see any CCC, not just ENTDAA (see Section
553   5.1.9.3.4), and shall behave as follows:
554     • The Slave shall appropriately process all required Broadcast CCCs, including ENTDAA, RSTDAA
555       (see Section 5.1.9.3.3), ENEC (see Section 5.1.9.3.1), and DISEC (see Section 5.1.9.3.1).
556       Examples of appropriate processing:
557        • RSTDAAA has no effect, since no Dynamic Address is assigned
558        • If the Slave would never issue a Master Request, then DISEC for Master Request can be
559           ignored.
560    • The Slave shall recognize the CCCs ENTHDR0 through ENTHDR7 (see Section 5.1.9.3.9), and
561      then wait for the HDR Exit Pattern.
562    • The Slave may choose to understand and process the SETDASA CCC (see Section 5.1.9.3.10)
563      when its Static Address matches.
564    • The Slave may choose to understand and process the SETAASA CCC (see Section 5.1.9.3.22).
565    • The Slave shall disregard all Directed CCC commands, but shall properly recognize the ends of
566      Directed CCCs (either repeated START followed by 7’h7E, or STOP).
567    • When no Dynamic Address has been assigned yet, the Slave may either support or ignore non-
568      Required and Conditionally Required Broadcast CCCs.
569      This is true even if the Slave supports any of these CCCs after being assigned a Dynamic Address.
570      For example, the Slave may choose to only support Test Mode only when no Dynamic Address is
571      assigned.
572    • The I3C Slave shall ignore S0 type errors related to incorrect addresses only (see
573      Section 5.1.10.1.1).




                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                 29
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
      Specification for I3C Basic                                                                      Version 1.0
                                                                                                      19-Jul-2018

574   The role of an I3C Slave shall be as follows:
575   1. Following a START or a Repeated START, at any speed conforming to Section 6 of this
576       Specification, the I3C Slave shall attempt to match the Address to the I3C Broadcast Address
577       (7’h7E) or to its own Dynamic Address, once assigned. If a match is found, then the I3C Slave
578       shall treat that Message as I3C SDR.
579   2. If the Message is addressed to the Slave’s Dynamic Address, then the Slave may ACK or passively
580       NACK the Address Header:
581       a. If the Slave ACKs the Address Header, then the Slave shall process the Message as I3C SDR,
582            following all rules as outlined in this Section.
583       b. If the Slave NACKs the Address Header (does not drive the ACK bit Low), then the Slave
584            may disregard any bits that follow, up until the next Repeated START or STOP.
585   3. If the Message is addressed to the I3C Broadcast Address (7’h7E), with a Write (RnW bit is 0),
586       then the Slave shall process that Message at least through the first byte of data (if any data is
587       present in the Message):
588       a. If there is a byte of data in a 7’h7E Broadcast Message, then the Message is a CCC (Common
589            Command Code) Command, per Section 5.1.9.
590       b. The I3C Slave shall process all applicable Required CCC commands, per Section 5.1.9.3. A
591            command may be either Always Required, or only Contextually Required, per Table 9.
592       c. If the CCC command changes the Mode of the I3C Bus, then the I3C Slave shall handle the
593            new Mode in one of two ways.
594            Either:
595            i. If the new Mode is Dynamic Address Assignment Mode (see Section 5.1.4), and required
596                for all I3C Slaves, then the Slave shall participate if it does not have a current Dynamic
597                Address; otherwise, the Slave shall await the STOP that indicates exit from Dynamic
598                Address Assignment Mode.
599              Or:
600              ii. If the new Mode is HDR (High Data Rate) Mode, then the Slave may either enter into
601                   HDR Mode if it supports that specific HDR Mode, or else enable its HDR Exit Pattern
602                   detector (per Section 5.2.1 and Section 5.2.1.3) to await the exit from HDR Mode.
603   4.   If the Message is not addressed to the I3C Broadcast Address (7’h7E) or to the Slave’s Dynamic
604        Address, then the I3C Slave shall await either a Repeated START or a STOP. The Slave may
605        record/monitor the bits as they pass (if desired), but the only obligation is to wait for either a
606        Repeated START or a STOP:
607         • A Repeated START is defined by the SDA line changing from High to Low while the SCL line
608             is High, per Section 6.
609         • A STOP is indicated by the SDA line changing from Low to High while the SCL line is High,
610             per Section 6.
611        In both cases, I3C timing may or may not be the same as with I2C.




      30                              Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                 All rights reserved.
                                                    Confidential
      Version 1.0                                                                   Specification for I3C Basic
      19-Jul-2018

      5.1.2.2        I3C Address Header
612   The I3C Address Header follows either a START, or a Repeated START. The format is the same as I2C: 7
613   bits of Address, 1 bit of RnW, and 1 bit of ACK/NACK.
614   The Address Header following a START is an Arbitrable Address Header, as explained in Section 5.1.2.2.1.
615   This means the START and at least the first Address bit and ACK/NACK are issued on SDA using Open
616   Drain Bus drive, similar to I2C. However, some of the Arbitrable Address Header may be driven on SDA
617   using Push-Pull and higher speed (see Section 5.1.2.2.2).
618   The Address Header following a Repeated START is always driven on SDA using Push-Pull, with the
619   exception of the ACK/NACK (see Section 5.1.2.2.4).
620   Using the I3C Arbitrable Address Header, I3C Slaves may transmit any of three requests to the I3C Master:
621   1. An In-Band Interrupt, per Section 5.1.6. This is equivalent to toggling a wire to get the Master’s
622       attention. The In-Band Interrupt request shall be made using the Slave’s Dynamic Address with a
623       RnW bit of 1.
624   2. A Secondary Master request, per Section 5.1.7. An I3C Slave shall not make such a request unless
625       it is marked as a Secondary Master in its BCR register, per Section 5.1.1.2.1. The Secondary
626       Master request shall be made using the Slave’s Dynamic Address with a RnW bit of 0.
627   3. A Hot-Join request, per Section 5.1.5. An I3C Slave shall only make such a request when
628       becoming available after the I3C Bus is operational. The Hot-Join request shall be made using the
629       special Hot-Join Address of 7’h02.
630   The I3C Slaves shall make these requests to the I3C Master in only two Bus conditions:
631   1. A START (but not a Repeated START) is issued on the Bus following a Bus Available Condition,
632       per Section 5.1.3. The Slave may transmit its Dynamic Address or the Hot-Join Address (7’h02)
633       following the START, by adhering to the I3C Address Arbitration rules per Section 5.1.2.2.1.
634   2. The Bus is in a Bus Available Condition, per Section 5.1.3, so the Slave may issue a START by
635       pulling the SDA Low.
636       a. If the Slave pulls the SDA Low, then the Master shall pull SCL Low within a best-efforts
637            period of time, where that time is not explicitly defined.
638       b. The Master shall also pull SDA Low (overlapping the Slave pulling it Low).
639       c. Once the Master has pulled the SCL Low, the Slave shall control the SDA line in Open Drain
640            mode (i.e., either pull Low, or release High).
641       d. The Slave may then issue its Address in the normal way (condition 1 above).




                                     Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                               31
                                                All rights reserved.
                                                   Confidential
      Specification for I3C Basic                                                                         Version 1.0
                                                                                                          19-Jul-2018

      5.1.2.2.1       I3C Address Arbitration
642   An Address Header following a START (but not a Repeated START) is subject to Arbitration, meaning both
643   the Master and one or more Slaves may attempt to drive an Address onto the Bus, using SDA. Such Address
644   Headers are defined as Arbitrable Address Headers.
645   The Arbitration model follows the common Open Drain approach. All Devices (whether Master or Slave)
646   that are transmitting an Address shall then follow the same rule:
647   1. If the current bit to transmit is a 0, then the Device shall drive SDA Low after the falling edge of
648        SCL and hold Low until the next falling edge of SCL.
649        Note:
650        Other Devices may also be driving SDA Low, but that is acceptable.
651   2.   If the current bit to transmit is a 1, then the Device shall not drive SDA, but rather shall High-Z
652        SDA on the falling edge of SCL.
653        a. Additionally, the Device shall monitor the SDA on the rising edge of SCL to determine
654             whether another Device has driven SDA Low.
655        b. If another Device has driven the SDA Low, then the Device has “lost” the Arbitration and
656             shall not further participate in this Address Header. That is, the Device shall not transmit any
657             more bits, but may wait for a future START Condition (but not a Repeated START
658             Condition).

      5.1.2.2.2       I3C Address Arbitration Optimization
659   I3C Address Arbitration may optionally be optimized, as detailed in this Section.
660   As previously described in Section 5.1.2.2, an I3C Master Device assigns 7-bit Dynamic Addresses with
661   values in the range 7’h03 to 7’h7B. However because the I3C Master treats the entire 9-bit Arbitrable Address
662   Header as Open Drain, it has no way to detect whether a Slave Device might be transmitting its own Address
663   during some (or all) of the Address Header.
664   Note that for I3C Secondary Masters and I3C In-Band Interrupt Slaves, the I3C Master is free to restrict
665   assigned Dynamic Addresses to the lower half of the available range (7’h03 to 7’h3F), thus leaving the value
666   of Address bit A6 (the first Address bit after the START) as value 0 in all assigned Dynamic Addresses.
667   Having restricted Dynamic Addresses in this manner, the I3C Master may then optionally optimize the
668   Arbitrable Address Header as follows:
669    • If the I3C Master is transmitting a 1 value (i.e. High-Z on SDA), as it would when transmitting
670      7’h7E, then it can monitor SDA on the rising edge of SCL. If SDA has the value 1 (i.e. if SDA is
671      not being driven by any Slave), then the I3C Master may optionally transmit the remainder of the
672      Address Header (up until the ACK/NACK) in Push-Pull mode. See Figure 10, upper waveform.
673    • If the I3C Master is transmitting a 0 value (i.e. is driving SDA Low), or if SDA was driven Low by
674      a Slave, then the I3C Master shall transmit the remainder of the Address Header using Open Drain.
675    • If the I3C Master intends to transmit solely to other I3C Devices (i.e. not to any I2C Devices), then
676      it may optionally maintain the SCL pulse width below 50ns, with the result that any I2C Devices
677      present on the Bus will see only a 0 value. This shorter pulse width produces a higher data rate,
678      because for Open Drain Arbitration only the Low time is extended. See Figure 10, middle
679      waveform.
680    • If the I3C Master intends to transmit to any I2C Devices, then it must use the slower I2C timing.
681      See Figure 10, lower waveform.




      32                               Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
      Version 1.0                                                                              Specification for I3C Basic
      19-Jul-2018

682   The upper waveform of Figure 10 illustrates this optimization. When the I3C Master sees a value of 1 for
683   Address bit A6, but previously made sure that all Dynamic Addresses assigned to I3C In-Band Interrupt
684   Slaves have a 0 in bit A6, then the I3C Master knows that the line was not being driven by any such Slave.

         SCL

      5.1.2.2.3       Consequence of Master Starting a Frame with an I3C Slave Address
687   The I3C Master normally should start a Frame with 7’h7E (for all I3C Messages) or an I2C Static Address
688   (when sending only to a Legacy I2C Slave).
689   In both cases the Address may be arbitrated, and so the Master shall monitor to see whether an IBI, Mastership
690   request (Slave requests to become the Master), or Hot-Join request has been made.
691     • If not, then the Master may proceed normally.
692     • If so, then the Master may ACK or NACK that request and then proceed accordingly.
693   If the Master chooses to start an I3C Message with an I3C Dynamic Address, then special provisions shall
694   be made because that same I3C Slave may be initiating an IBI or Mastership request. So, one of three things
695   may happen:
696   1. The Addresses match, but the difference is caught on the RnW bit, and the Master was Writing
697        (RnW=0); so the Master wins (IBI with RnW=1 loses), and proceeds normally.
698   2. The Addresses match, but the difference is caught on the RnW bit, and the Master was Reading
699        (RnW=1); so the Master loses, and must ACK or NACK the Mastership request (RnW=0).
700   3. The Addresses match as do the RnW and so neither Master nor Slave shall ACK since both are
701        expecting the other side to.
702        a. This is a problem since the Master cannot tell whether the NACK was due to this condition,
703            or simply because the Slave may have chosen to NACK it.
704        b. The Master shall transmit the Slave Address again after a Repeated START (the next one or
705            any one prior to a STOP in the Frame). This allows it to determine which condition occurred
706            and to avoid a deadlock.

                                         Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                     33
                                                    All rights reserved.
                                                       Confidential
      Specification for I3C Basic                                                                   Version 1.0
                                                                                                    19-Jul-2018

      5.1.2.2.4      Address Header Following a Repeated START is Push-Pull
707   The Address transmitted by the I3C Master following a Repeated START shall not be arbitrated. That is, no
708   I3C Slave shall attempt to transmit is own Dynamic Address nor the Hot-Join Address following a Repeated
709   START.
710   As a result, the Address Header (i.e., the 7 bits of Address plus the RnW bit) shall be transmitted on SDA
711   using Push-Pull mode when the Message is not to a Legacy I2C Slave. The ACK/NACK bit that follows the
712   RnW bit is always Open Drain to allow the Slave to ACK or passively NACK its Address.

      5.1.2.2.5      I3C Slave Address Restrictions
713   The I3C Slave Address space is dependent on decisions by the Master. That is, the Master may choose the
714   Dynamic Addresses from a set of values, observing optional and non-optional restrictions as follows. These
715   restrictions are also illustrated in Table 9.
716     • The I3C Master shall not use any of 7’h00, 7’h01, 7’h02, 7’h7E, 7’h7F. All are reserved for I3C.
717     • The I3C Master shall not use any of 7’h3E, 7’h5E, 7’h6E, 7’h76, 7’h7A, 7’h7C, 7’h7F. All are
718       prohibited for detecting an error in the Broadcast Address (7’h7E).
719     • The I3C Master may choose to not use 7’h03, marked in I2C as reserved.
720     • The I3C Master shall not use 7’h04, 7’h05, 7’h06, 7’h07 if any Legacy I2C Devices are present on
721       the Bus that support I2C “High-Speed Mode”.
722     • The I3C Master shall not use 7’h7C or 7’h7D if any Legacy I2C Devices are present on the Bus
723       that support I2C Device ID Mode.
724     • The I3C Master shall not use 7’h78, 7’h79, 7’h7A, 7’h7B if any Legacy I2C Devices are present on
725       the Bus that support I2C Extended Address Mode and have an Extended Address or would be
726       impacted by the Extended Address mechanism.
