      5.1.2.3         I3C SDR Data Words
728   In I3C SDR, the Data Words match I2C only in the sense that they are both 9 bits long. I3C SDR Data Words
729   differ from I2C in three ways, as detailed in Sub-Sections 5.1.2.3.1, 5.1.2.3.2, and 5.1.2.3.4.
730   In summary:
731   1. Handoff from Address ACK to SDR Master Write Data: When performing an SDR Write, the
732        handoff from the Slave’s Address Header ACK to the Master’s first Data bit is different in I3C.
733        I2C is Open Drain, so overlap from the ACK Low into the first bit is harmless. By contrast, I3C is
734        Push-Pull and so this handoff is specified (see Section 5.1.2.3.1).
735   2. Ninth Bit of SDR Master Written Data as Parity: In I2C, the ninth Data bit written by the
736        Master is an ACK by the Slave. By contrast, in I3C the ninth Data bit written by the Master is the
737        Parity of the preceding eight Data bits. Therefore, in I3C the Slave shall not drive the SDA line for
738        Data written by the Master in SDR. In SDR terms, the ninth bit of Write data is referred to as the
739        T-Bit (for ‘Transition’) (see Section 5.1.2.3.2).
740   3. Ninth Bit of SDR Slave Returned (Read) Data as End-of-Data: In I2C, the ninth Data bit from
741        Slave to Master is an ACK by the Master. By contrast, in I3C this bit allows the Slave to end a
742        Read, and allows the Master to Abort a Read. In SDR terms, the ninth bit of Read data is referred
743        to as the T-Bit (for ‘Transition’) (see Section 5.1.2.3.4).

      5.1.2.3.1       Transition from Address ACK to SDR Master Write Data
744   The end of any Address Header (whether Arbitrated or not) is an ACK or NACK by the one or more addressed
745   Slaves, using Open Drain on SDA:
746     • If 7’h7E, then it is the ACK of all I3C Slaves on the Bus.
747     • If a single Slave Address, then it is the ACK (or NACK) of the addressed Slave, or a NACK if no
748       such Slave is on the Bus.
749   When the Address Header results in an ACK, and the Message is SDR Write from Master, the SDA line has
750   to be turned from Open Drain to Push-Pull for the first data bit. To do that safely, I3C SDR specifies how the
751   handoff is to occur. This is summarized below and shown in Figure 32.
752   1. The I3C Slave shall hold the SDA line Low during the ACK (while SCL is Low).
753         • This is an Open Drain SCL Low period.
754   2. After the I3C Slave sees the rising edge of SCL, it releases the SDA line to High-Z.
755         • The I3C Slave shall release the SDA line using normal (Push-Pull) timing (release the SDA line
756            as soon as it sees SCL rising).
757   3. After the rising edge of SCL, the I3C Master shall drive the SDA line Low.
758         • As a result, both Master and Slave will be driving the SDA line Low for a short overlap (which
759            is safe).
760         • The SCL High period may be as short as the minimal tDIG_H, per Section 6.2.
761   4. On the falling edge of SCL the I3C Master shall begin driving data on the SDA line, using Push-
762        Pull drive as shown in Figure 32.
763   When the Address Header results in a NACK, the Master may choose to either:
764   1. Continue the transaction, by generating a Repeated START
765   or
766   2. Relinquish the Bus, by generating a STOP as shown in Figure 33.




      36                              Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                 All rights reserved.
                                                    Confidential
      Version 1.0                                                                         Specification for I3C Basic
      19-Jul-2018

      5.1.2.3.2       Transition from Address ACK to Mandatory Byte during IBI
767   The end of any IBI Address Header during an IBI request (whether the Address Header is Arbitrated or not)
768   is either an ACK or a NACK by the Master, using Open Drain on SDA.
769       If the Master detects one of the Slave Addresses, and if the Master chooses to receive the request,
770       then the Master will ACK the request per Section 5.1.6.2.
771    • ACK: When the IBI Slave Address Header results in an ACK, and the Slave Device is capable of
772      sending a Mandatory Byte, then the SDA line has to be turned from Open Drain to Push-Pull for
773      the first data bit. To do that safely, I3C SDR specifies how the handoff is to occur. This is
774      summarized below.
775     1. The I3C Master shall hold the SDA line Low during the ACK (i.e., while the SCL line is Low).
776           This is an Open Drain SCL Low period.
777     2. After the rising edge of the SCL line, the I3C Slave shall drive the SDA line Low as soon as
778           possible. As a result, both Master and Slave will be driving the SDA line Low for a short overlap
779           (which is safe).
780     3. After (A) a minimum of the tSCO time reported by the Slave Device, or (B) a predetermined safe
781           time that could guarantee the takeover by all Slaves, the I3C Master shall release the SDA line.
782           The SCL High period may be as short as the minimal tDIG_H, per Section 6.2.
783     4. On the falling edge of SCL, the I3C Slave shall begin driving data on the SDA line, using Push-
784           Pull drive.
785    • NACK: When the Address Header results in a NACK, the Master may choose to either:
786        • Continue the transaction, by generating a Repeated START, or
787        • Relinquish the Bus by generating a STOP.

      5.1.2.3.3       Ninth Bit of SDR Master Written Data as Parity
788   The ninth data bit of each SDR Data Word written by the I3C Master (also referred to as the T-Bit) is a Parity
789   bit, calculated using odd parity. Parity can help in detecting noise-caused errors on the line. The value of this
790   Parity bit shall be the XOR of the 8 Data bits with 1, i.e.: XOR( Data [7:0], 1 ).
791   Examples:
792    • If all eight data bits are 1’s (0xFF), then the Parity bit value will be 1.
793    • If all eight data bits are 0’s (0x00), then the Parity bit value will be 1.
794    • If an odd number of bits in the Data Word are 1’s (e.g. 0xFE or 0x01), then the Parity bit value will
795      be 0.
796   T (Parity) bit writes shall always be kept valid through the SCL High period. In the case of a T-Bit
797   representing the last data byte, the write is therefore kept valid through the SCL High period, and the next
798   SCL Low can then be used to either change the SDA, or not change the SDA, in preparation for the Repeated
799   START or STOP that follows.




                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                    37
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
      Specification for I3C Basic                                                                         Version 1.0
                                                                                                         19-Jul-2018

      5.1.2.3.4       Ninth Bit of SDR Slave Returned (Read) Data as End-of-Data
800   In I2C, Read from Slave has the issue that only the Master ends the Read, so the Slave has no ability to control
801   the amount of data it returns. In I3C SDR, by contrast, the Slave controls the number of data Words it returns;
802   but it also allows the I3C Master to abort the Read prematurely when necessary.
803   This mechanism is controlled purely by the ninth (T) Data bit of each SDR Data Word returned by the I3C
804   Slave. The ninth bit is returned by the Slave in one of three ways, as explained below.
805     • The I3C Slave returns the ninth bit as 0 (SDA Low) to end the Message:
806        • The Slave shall set SDA Low on the falling edge of SCL.
807        • On the following rising edge of SCL, the Slave shall set SDA to High-Z.
808        • The I3C Master shall drive SDA Low on the rising edge of SCL, thereby overlapping with the
809           Slave.
810        • The I3C Master then shall issue either a STOP as shown in Figure 39, or a Repeated START as
811           shown in Figure 40 (on the next clock, or one after, per the normal I2C procedure for setting
812           up SDA to issue a Repeated START).
813     • The I3C Slave returns the ninth bit as 1 (SDA High) to continue the Message (and permit the
814       Master to abort the Message):
815        • The Slave shall set SDA High on the falling edge of SCL.
816        • On the following rising edge of SCL, the Slave shall set SDA to High-Z, thereby Parking the
817           Bus for the SCL High period:
818              • If the I3C Master is able to continue the reply from the Slave, then it shall do nothing. The
819                weak Pull-Up resistor on SDA will keep SDA High during the SCL High period, as shown
820                in Figure 41.
821              • If the I3C Master wants to abort the Message, then it shall drive SDA Low after the rising
822                edge of SCL, thereby terminating the Message with a Repeated START. The I3C Master
823                then takes control starting with the falling edge of SCL. The Master shall ensure enough
824                delay after SCL rising before driving SDA Low to ensure no contention. In order to
825                achieve this delay, the Master might have to extend the SCL High period.
826                Since the transition of SDA Low when SCL is High is a Repeated START, the Master
827                may begin a new Address (see Figure 43), or it may issue a STOP in the next cycle (see
828                Figure 42). However in a Mixed Bus the Master should extend the SCL Low period, in
829                order to ensure that any Spike Filters for Legacy I2C Devices properly reset.
830        • The Slave shall monitor the SDA on the falling edge of SCL:
831              • If SDA is High, then the Slave shall continue with the next data value.
832              • If SDA is Low (i.e., if there has been a Repeated START), then the Message has been
833                aborted, and the Slave shall not drive SDA after that.




      38                               Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
      Version 1.0                                                                          Specification for I3C Basic
      19-Jul-2018

      5.1.2.4         Use of Clock Speed to Prevent Legacy I2C Devices from Seeing I3C Traffic
834   In a system, there are three possible I3C Bus Configurations:
835   1. Pure Bus: Only I3C Devices are present on the Bus.
836   2. Mixed Fast Bus: Both I3C Devices and Legacy I2C Devices are present on the Bus, such that the
837        Legacy I2C Devices are restricted to ones that are generally permissible (i.e., Slave-only, and no
838        Slave clock stretching), and that have a true I2C 50 ns Spike Filter on SCL. (I.e., I2C Devices that
839        do not “see” the SCL line as High when the High duration is less than 50 ns, across all
840        temperatures and processes.)
841   3. Mixed Slow/Limited Bus: Both I3C Devices and Legacy I2C Devices are present on the Bus,
842        such that the Legacy I2C Devices are restricted to ones that are generally permissible (i.e., Slave-
843        only, and no Slave clock stretching), but that do not have a true I2C 50 ns Spike Filter on SCL.
844   The Bus designer’s choice of Bus Configuration affects what speed options are available for I3C SDR, as
845   well as what HDR Modes are possible at various clock speeds. Table 10 shows the possible options for each
846   Bus Configuration.
847      Table 10 Available Options for Bus Operating Parameters, Per I3C Bus Configuration

                                                    Available Options Per Bus Configuration
         Bus Operating
          Parameter                                                                               Mixed Slow and
                                  Pure Bus                      Mixed Fast Bus
                                                                                                   Limited Bus
       SDR Mode Speed                                       I2C messages: Fm or Fm+,
                                                          I3C messages: SCL High from
                               fSCL (Min) to fSCL
                                                      tDIG_H_MIXED (Min) to tDIG_H_MIXED (Max)    Fm or Fm+ only1
                                     (Max)
                                                          with SCL low up to tDIG_L (Max)
                                                              (see Section 5.1.2.4.1)
       Note:
           1)   May be faster if all Legacy I2C Devices are index 1, per Table 5




                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                    39
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
      Specification for I3C Basic                                                                           Version 1.0
                                                                                                            19-Jul-2018

      5.1.2.4.1       Use of Duty Cycle to Achieve Lower Effective Speed in a Mixed Fast Bus
848   A pure I3C Bus may simply change the clock speed to any frequency in the allowed speed range, as outlined
849   in Section 6. By contrast, a Mixed Fast Bus wanting to clock faster than the slowest Legacy I2C Device needs
850   to take advantage of the Spike Filter, i.e, shall ensure that the SCL High period is shorter than the Spike Filter,
851   in order to prevent the Legacy I2C Devices from seeing the SCL High period (see tDIG_H_MIXED in Table 59).
852   As a result, the Legacy I2C Device ‘sees’ the SCL as staying Low the whole period.
853   However, a Mixed Fast Bus I3C Master can change the effective Bus frequency by varying the duty-cycle of
854   SCL. This allows running the data rate slower, for example to accommodate Slaves which need a lower rate
855   as defined by GETMXDS CCC (see Section 5.1.9.3.18). It may also be necessary to accommodate Legacy
856   I2C Devices with Spike Filters that need a longer Low period in order to function properly.
857   In this model the SCL High period shall never exceed tDIG_H_MIXED, thus staying below the 50ns required by
858   the I2C Spike Filter; however, the Low period is free to be any length permitted by I3C’s allowed clock
859   frequency range. For example, depending on the clock generation capability of the I3C Master, the SCL Low
860   period may be a multiple of the High period, or it may be any multiple of some higher frequency clock
861   capable of providing a High period less than or equal to tDIG_H_MIXED, but greater than the minimum SCL High
862   period as defined in Table 57.
863   Example 1: An SCL High period of 40ns, plus a Low period of 280ns, yields a total clock period of 320ns
864   which corresponds to a frequency of 3.125MHz.
865   Example 2: In order to ensure that the Legacy I2C Device Spike Filters continue tracking SCL as Low, an
866   SCL High period of 40ns could be used with a Low period of 80ns, yielding a total clock period of 120ns
867   which corresponds to a frequency of 8.3MHz.
868   Such adjustment of the clock Duty Cycle brings three benefits:
869    • It remains hidden from Legacy I2C Devices, while still significantly increasing the SDR data rate.
870    • It ensures a longer Low period, so that Legacy I2C Device Spike Filters continue to track SCL as
871      Low.
872    • It is easy for a Master to generate, and has no impact on I3C Slaves (which just react to clock
873      edges).
874   This model works because all I3C Slaves must be able to accommodate 12.5MHz as a clock rate, so the
875   shorter High period will not impact them.
876   Note that this Duty Cycle technique does not resolve issues of long Buses with high line capacitance. This is
877   because the short High period may be insufficient for SDA propagation, even if the Low period is long
878   enough.




      40                                Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                   All rights reserved.
                                                      Confidential
      Version 1.0                                                                         Specification for I3C Basic
      19-Jul-2018

      5.1.2.5         Master Clock Stalling
879   In SDR Mode the I3C Master may Stall the I3C Bus during the SCL Low period, but only under the specific,
880   transitory conditions described in this Section.
881   Stalling may be necessary for either of two reasons:
882   1. The absolute or relative timing of a Message to a specific Slave, or to all Slaves, needs to be
883        carefully controlled. Clock Stalling provides the Master with fine-grained data timing control.
884   2. The I3C Master needs to internally synchronize data. This may be due to parts of the Master’s
885        system waking up in response to data, to changing state, or to otherwise needing time during a
886        transaction.
887   Note that Stalling impacts Bus performance. For example, it will reduce the Bus capacity and increase the
888   latency of any Devices issuing In-Band Interrupts.
889   To Stall the Bus, the I3C Master shall hold SCL Low while the Bus is in one of the four following conditions,
890   each of which is detailed below:
891   1. I3C/I2C Transfer, ACK/NACK Phase
892   2. Write Data Transfer, Parity Bit
893   3. I3C Read Transfer, Transition Bit
894   4. Dynamic Address Assignment, First Bit of Assigned Address
895   The maximum Stall time (SCL Low period) shall be 15 ms; note, this is an absolute maximum. The Master
896   should use the shortest Stall duration possible given current circumstances, as per Table 11.
897                                      Table 11 Master Clock Stall Times
                                                                                Section            Maximum
                                    Condition
                                                                                                   Stall Time
           I3C/I2C Transfer, ACK/NACK Phase                                     5.1.2.5.1            100 µs
           Write Data Transfer, Parity Bit                                      5.1.2.5.2            100 µs
           I3C Read Transfer, Transition Bit                                    5.1.2.5.3            100 µs
           Dynamic Address Assignment, First Bit of Assigned Address            5.1.2.5.4            15 ms

898   In all cases, after Stalling the SCL Low period, the continuation of the clock (i.e., the first SCL rising edge
899   after the extended Low period) shall follow the normal rules for I3C SDR (for I3C SDR Messages), or for
900   I2C (for I2C Messages) including rise time, change in SDA (if any) before SCL rise, next bit, etc. For example,
901   the I3C Master might choose to terminate the Frame after this Stalled bit with a STOP; but in this case the
902   Master is required to observe the requirements for STOP timing, as appropriate.
903   It is recommended to use Master Clock Stalling only when necessary and unavoidable. In all other
904   circumstances the Master should avoid Master Clock Stalling because of its negative impacts on Bus
905   performance. In order to help guide system designers, Data Sheets for I3C Master Devices should include
906   appropriately detailed SCL Stalling parameters.




                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                  41
                                                  All rights reserved.

                                                                                                  19-Jul-2018

      5.1.2.5.1      I3C/I2C Transfer, ACK/NACK Phase
907   Master Clock Stalling during the ACK/NACK phase of the I3C/I2C transfer (see Figure 11) indicates one of
908   the following:
909     • The Master can allow the I3C/I2C Slave to prepare to receive data (for write transfers) or transmit
910       data (for read transfers)
911     • The Master can Stall SCL during write or read transfers to Legacy I2C Slaves in case of underrun
912       and overflow situations
913     • The Master can Stall the clock during the ACK/NACK phase of the incoming In-Band Interrupt
914       (Slave Interrupt Request or Mastership Request), to decide whether to ACK or NACK depending
915       upon the incoming In-Band Interrupt
916     • The Master can allow the Slave to respond with data for the directed GET CCC commands if the
917       Slave is not ready with the CCC data to be returned

      5.1.3      Bus Conditions
943   This Specification defines Open Drain Pull-Up and High-Keeper, as well as three distinct conditions in which
944   the I3C Bus shall be considered inactive: Bus Free, Bus Available, and Bus Idle.

      5.1.3.1        Open Drain Pull-Up and High-Keeper
945   I3C Master Devices shall provide an active Open Drain class Pull-Up, to be engaged whenever the Bus is in
946   Open Drain mode (with exceptions, detailed below, where a weak Pull-Up may be used).
947   This active Pull-Up shall be implemented either:
948   1. As a passive resistance from VDD, or
949   2. As a passive resistance from a current source, or
950   3. In any other method that both:
951       a. Balances its current sourcing in order to ensure that SDA rises within trDA (see Table 57), and
952       b. Is not so strong as to prevent a Slave with the minimum IOL driver (see Table 54) from driving
953            SDA Low within trDA (see Table 58).
954   In addition to the active Open Drain class Pull-Up, a High-Keeper is also required on the Bus. A High-Keeper
955   is generally used for a Master-to-Slave or Slave-to-Master handoff, and for optional termination uses where
956   the Master can signal a termination by driving SDA Low while parked High.
957   The High-Keeper on the Bus shall be strong enough to prevent system leakage (i.e., the sum of the leakage
958   of all Devices on the Bus) from pulling SDA, and sometimes SCL, Low. The High-Keeper on the Bus shall
959   also be weak enough that a Slave with the minimum IOL driver (see Table 54) is able to pull SDA, SCL, or
960   both Low within the Minimum tDIG_L period.
961   The Master may provide the High-Keeper on the Bus. If the Master does so, then the Master shall also provide
962   a way to disable its High-Keeper. Reasons to disable a Master-provided High-Keeper might include that the
963   High-Keeper is not strong enough for the present system leakage, or other reasons.
964   A Master-provided High-Keeper may optionally be implemented using a single, common Pull-Up Device
965   capable of supporting both the active Open Drain class Pull-Up function and the weak High-Keeper class
966   Pull-Up function.
967   Whether implemented as a single combined Pull-Up, or as two separate Pull-Ups, the Master should switch
968   SCL and SDA, each independently, between three Pull-Up states as needed, based on Bus state:
969   1. No Pull-Up (High-Z)
970   2. High-Keeper Pull-Up
971   3. Open Drain Pull-Up
972   The Bus shall have High-Keepers on SDA and SCL. When adequate High-Keepers cannot be provided by
973   the Master, then the system designer is required to handle this externally. Reasons why the Master would be
974   unable to provide adequate High-Keepers might include that the Master does not support High-Keepers, that
975   the Master-provided High-Keepers are not strong enough for present needs, or that the Bus is too long to use
976   the Master-provided High-Keepers.
977   The system High-Keepers on SCL and SDA may be implemented as one or more passive resistors tied to
978   VDD, or they may be active Bus-Keeper Devices that turn off when the respective line is pulled below some
979   threshold. The system High-Keepers on SCL and SDA shall be sized so as to balance between system leakage
980   and the requirement that I3C Devices be able to pull the corresponding line Low within the tDIG_L period.
981   Note:
982       The Master may choose to not use the Open Drain class Pull-Up in cases of Open Drain mode where
983       the SDA happens to be already High (i.e., is coming into the SCL Falling edge). In that case, the
984       Master may choose to rely solely upon the High-Keeper, in order to use less power if and when a
985       Slave drives SDA Low.



                                     Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                 47
                                                All rights reserved.
                                                   Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                        Version 1.0
                                                                                                         19-Jul-2018

       5.1.3.2         Bus Free Condition
986    The Bus Free Condition is defined as a period occurring after a STOP and before a START, and with the
987    following duration:
988      • For Pure Bus: A duration of at least tCAS (see Table 58)
989      • For Mixed Bus (i.e., at least one Legacy I2C Device is present on the I3C Bus): A duration of at
990        least tBUF (see Table 57)

       5.1.3.3         Bus Available Condition
991    The Bus Available Condition is defined as a period during which the Bus Free Condition is sustained
992    continuously for a duration of at least tAVAL (see Table 58). A Slave may only issue a START Request (e.g.,
993    for an In-Band Interrupt, or for a Master Handoff Request) after a Bus Available Condition.

       5.1.3.4         Bus Idle Condition
994    The I3C Bus Idle Condition is defined in order to help ensure Bus stability during Hot-Join events. The Bus
995    Idle Condition is defined as a period during which the Bus Available Condition (see Section 5.1.3.3) is
996    sustained continuously for a duration of at least tIDLE (see Table 58).
997    Note:
 998        If a Hot-Join Device is powered up onto the I3C Bus at the same time as the Main Master, then the
 999        Hot-Join Device may pull SDA Low after 1 ms if (1) the Main Master has SCL and SDA pulled up,
1000        and (2) the Master does not act on the I3C Bus within the same Idle period.

       5.1.3.5         Activity States
1001   I3C provides a mechanism for the Master to inform Slaves about expected upcoming levels of activity on the
1002   I3C Bus, in order to help the Slaves better manage their internal states. Four Activity State levels from 0
1003   through 3 are defined (see Table 12).
1004                                            Table 12 Activity States

                         Activity State              Activity Interval                   CCC
                                0                       1 µ second                     ENTAS0
                                1                      100 µ second                    ENTAS1
                                2                      2 millisecond                   ENTAS2
                                3                      50 millisecond                  ENTAS3

1005   The Activity State number serves as a hint to the Slave, indicating how long it will be before the Master
1006   directs Bus activity to that Slave, and the likely latencies to expect in response to the Slave pulling SDA Low
1007   (i.e., for the START Request to then generate an In-Band Interrupt Request or a Master Handoff Request).
1008   The Master uses CCC commands ENTAS0, ENTAS1, ENTAS2, and ENTAS3 (see Section 5.1.9.3.2) to
1009   communicate the four expected Bus Activity states to Slaves. Each ENTASx CCC has both a Broadcast
1010   version and a Directed (per-Slave) version. The Master may switch to a different Activity State in any way,
1011   and at any time, by issuing the appropriate ENTSAx CCC command.
1012   The Slave may use the received Activity State hint to adjust internal settings such as power savings, FIFO
1013   trigger levels, timestamp counters and clock rates, and other suitable operating parameters. However Slaves
1014   are not required to support the Activity States CCCs (ENTASx), and as a result could even ignore them
1015   completely.
1016   The Activity States mechanism is a basis for a general agreement between Master and Slave, i.e., that the
1017   Slave may NACK any access occurring sooner than the general time factor. For example: If the Master sends
1018   the ENTAS2 CCC, meaning the Master is unlikely to initiate a request sooner than 2 ms from the time the
1019   CCC is sent, then a request arriving only 1 ms later could result in a NACK (although it will be ACKed if the

       48                              Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                       Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

1020   request is repeated 50us later, i.e., after the Slave re-awakens). As a result, the Slave shall wake up either
1021   upon any Bus activity, or upon matching 7’h7E and its own Dynamic Address.
1022   The Activity State CCCs also adjust the maximum value for I3C Bus timing parameter tCAS (Clock after
1023   START; see Table 58), the maximum amount of time that the Master may take to generate the SCL clock
1024   (drive SCL Low) in response to the Slave pulling SDA Low. Note that tCAS is only a worst-case number; it
1025   does not indicate whether or not the Master will ACK the In-Band Interrupt Request or Master Request.
1026   Further, the selected Activity State does not necessarily indicate the time at which the Master will read
1027   additional data from a Slave in response to an In-Band Interrupt; that is a private contract between Master
1028   and Slave. A Slave that does not support the ENTASn CCCs shall have a tCAS maximum value of 50
1029   milliseconds (the ENTAS3 value).
1030   Activity States are not intended as a substitute for a more precise power mode, nor for any other mechanism
1031   that might be supported by private contract between Master and Slave. For example, if a Slave has a device
1032   power mode setting, then the Master should use that mechanism to put the Slave into the desired state.
1033   Likewise, a Slave could provide a FIFO trigger level setting, relating to the amount of time that the Master
1034   will have to read the FIFO contents in response to an In-Band Interrupt Request; if so, then the Master should
1035   use that setting to match its internal latencies.

       5.1.4      Bus Initialization and Dynamic Address Assignment Mode
1036   As detailed in this Section, the Main Master is responsible for performing a Dynamic Address Assignment
1037   procedure, in order to provide a unique Dynamic Address to each Device connected to the I3C Bus.
1038   The Main Master shall provide a Dynamic Address to a Device:
1039   1. Upon any initialization of the I3C Bus, and
1040   2. When the Device is connected to an already configured I3C Bus.
1041   Once a Device receives a Dynamic Address, that Dynamic Address shall be used in all of that Device’s
1042   subsequent transactions on the I3C Bus, until and unless the Master changes the Device’s Dynamic Address.
1043   The only way for the Master to change the Device’s Dynamic Address is by using either the RSTDA CCC
1044   command (see Section 5.1.9.3.3) or the SETNEWDA CCC command (see Section 5.1.9.3.11). The Master
1045   might choose to change the Device’s Dynamic Address due to re-prioritization.
1046   The Main Master controls the Dynamic Address Assignment process. This process includes an Address
1047   Arbitration procedure similar to I2C’s (see [NXP01] at Sections 3.1 and 3.1.8). The I3C Arbitration procedure
1048   differs from I2C by using the values of the 48-bit Provisional ID and the Device’s I3C Characteristic Registers
1049   (that is, BCR and DCR), concatenated. The Device on the I3C Bus with the lowest concatenated value wins
1050   each Arbitration round in turn, and the Main Master assigns a unique Dynamic Address to each winning
1051   Device.




                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                   49
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                         Version 1.0
                                                                                                          19-Jul-2018

       5.1.4.1         Device Requirements for Dynamic Address Assignment

       5.1.4.1.1       Unique Identifiability
1052   In order to support the Dynamic Address Assignment procedure, each I3C Device to be connected to an I3C
1053   Bus shall be uniquely identifiable in one of two ways, before starting the procedure.
1054   Either:
1055   1. The Device may have a Static Address, in which case the Master may use that Static Address
1056       (presuming it is known to the Master).
1057        For example, an Address similar to what I2C specifies [NXP01].
1058   Or:
1059   2. The Device shall in all cases have a 48-bit Provisional ID. The Master shall use this 48-bit
1060       Provisional ID, unless the Device has a Static Address and the Master uses the Static Address.
1061        The 48-bit Provisional ID is composed of three parts:
1062        1. Bits [47:33]: MIPI Manufacturer ID [MIPI01] (15 bits)
1063                 Note: The Most Significant Bit of the MIPI Manufacturer ID is discarded, i.e.
1064                  only the 15 Least Significant Bits are used.
1065        2.   Bit [32]: Provisional ID Type Selector (One bit, 1’b1: Random Value, 1’b0: Vendor Fixed
1066             Value)
1067        3.   Bits [31:0]: 32 bits containing either a Vendor Fixed Value or a Random Value, depending on
1068             the value of Bit [32]:
1069             If the value of Bit [32] is 1’b0: Vendor Fixed Value:
1070               • Bits [31:16]: Part ID: The meaning of this 16-bit field is left to the Device vendor to
1071                 define.
1072               • Bits [15:12]: Instance ID: The value in this 4-bit field should identify the individual
1073                 Device, using a method selected by the system designer. For example: straps, fuses, non-
1074                 volatile memory, or another appropriate method.
1075               • Bits [11:0]: The meaning of this 12-bit field is left for definition with additional meaning.
1076                 For example: deeper Device Characteristics, which could optionally include Device
1077                 Characteristic Register values.
1078             If the value of Bit [32] is 1’b1: Random Value:
1079               • Bits [31:0]: 32-bit value randomly generated by the Device. This value can be queried via
1080                 General Test Mode, using the Command Code Enter Test Mode (ENTTM) (see
1081                 Section 5.1.9.3.8).
1082   Note:
1083        Under Vendor Test Mode, the Device may provide a fully random or pseudo-random 48-bit
1084        Provisional ID (see Section 5.1.9.3.8).

       5.1.4.2         Bus Initialization Sequence with Dynamic Address Assignment
1085   Bus Initialization and Dynamic Address Assignment shall be executed according to the following sequence.
1086   See also Figure 59 (Dynamic Address Assignment FSM).
1087   The Dynamic Address Assignment process shall be performed in Open Drain mode, except that the Repeated
1088   START and 7’h7E/R may be either Open Drain or Push-Pull. For Open Drain the Main Master shall drive
1089   the SCL line with clocks at the appropriate Open Drain speed for the Devices present on the I3C Bus. For
1090   Repeated START, the Main Master may optionally choose to actively drive the SDA line High, but only after
1091   the Slave has safely released the SDA line.



       50                               Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                   All rights reserved.
                                                      Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                      Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

1092   1.   The Main Master shall begin in an appropriately configured state, and shall have, either in its own
1093        non-volatile memory or as a result of having received it from the Application Host, the following
1094        data:
1095        a. The number of I3C compliant Devices that need to receive a Dynamic Address,
1096        b. The data for any I3C Devices resident on the I3C Bus that already have Static Addresses, and
1097        c. The data for any Legacy I2C Devices resident on the I3C Bus.
1098   2.   The Main Master shall assign Dynamic Addresses to any I3C Devices with a known Static
1099        Address, using the Command Code Set Dynamic Address from Static Address (SETDASA)
1100        (see Section 5.1.9.3.10), or by assigning all I3C Devices their known I2C Static Address using the
1101        Command Code Set All Addresses to Static Address (SETAASA) (see Section 5.1.9.3.22)
1102        Under these pre-conditions, Slaves supporting the SETAASA CCC and Slaves only supporting the
1103        SETDASA CCC (see Section 5.1.9.3.10) can be mixed on the I3C Bus. Via SETDASA, the
1104        Master will individually assign a Dynamic Address to each Slave not supporting SETAASA.
1105        In a system that mixes I2C-capable Devices and non-I2C-capable Devices, the Master shall send
1106        the SETAASA CCC before sending the ENTDAA CCC (see Section 5.1.9.3.4), in order to assign
1107        Dynamic Addresses to the I3C-only Slaves. In addition, any Slave having a restricted Address (see
1108        Table 9) shall not support SETAASA.
1109   3.   The Main Master shall send the Broadcast Command Code Enter Dynamic Address Assignment
1110        (ENTDAA) (see Section 5.1.9.3.4). There is no data associated with this Command Code.
1111   4.   The Main Master shall send a Repeated START, and the I3C Broadcast Address 7’h7E with RnW
1112        bit High (i.e. Read). Every I3C Device on the I3C Bus that does not yet have an assigned Dynamic
1113        Address, and that is not a Hot-Join Device, shall acknowledge the I3C Broadcast Address. (I.e.,
1114        Hot-Join Devices that do not yet have an assigned Dynamic Address shall not acknowledge the
1115        I3C Broadcast Address in this step.)
1116        At least one I3C Device on the I3C Bus will acknowledge the I3C Broadcast address in this step.
1117        Note:
1118            This use of the I3C Broadcast Address (7’h7E) with RnW bit High (i.e. Read) is specific to
1119            Dynamic Address Assignment Mode. It is also acceptable per the I2C Specification [NXP01].
1120   5.   The Main Master shall drive only the SCL line. The Main Master shall release the SDA line to a
1121        High-Z state, allowing SDA to go to High level via the Bus Pull-Up resistor.
1122   6.   Every I3C Device that responds to the I3C Broadcast Address sent in Step 4 shall drive the SDA
1123        line with its own 48-bit Provisional ID (using Big Endian bit order), until it loses Dynamic
1124        Address Arbitration.
1125        a. The 48-bit Provisional ID shall be transferred continuously, starting with the most significant
1126             bit (bit [47]), with no delimitation or ACK/NACK pulse.
1127        b. The Hot-Join Devices shall participate in the Dynamic Address Assignment procedure only
1128             after requesting it via an In-Band Interrupt using the Reserved Slave Address of 7’b0000_010
1129             and RnW bit Low (i.e. Write).
1130   7.   The Main Master shall continue to drive the SCL line with the same clock, while still releasing the
1131        SDA line. The I3C Device that did not yet lose the Arbitration shall then transfer its Bus
1132        Characteristics Register (BCR) and Device Characteristic Register(s) (DCR) per Section 5.1.1.2.2,
1133        until it eventually loses Dynamic Address Arbitration. See also Section 5.1.4.3.
1134   8.   The Device whose concatenated Provisional ID, BCR, and DCR has the lowest value will win the
1135        Arbitration round, due to the nature of Arbitration.
1136   Note:
1137        It is possible for multiple Devices to have the same concatenated value, although this is highly
1138        improbable. See Section 5.1.4.3.



                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                  51
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                                       Version 1.0
                                                                                                                        19-Jul-2018

1139   9.   The Main Master shall transfer a 7-bit wide Dynamic Address for the winning Device, in Open Drain
1140        Mode. This Dynamic Address shall incorporate the priority level that the Main Master assigns to the
1141        Device, per Section 5.1.6.2. The Arbitration-winning Device shall acknowledge the assigned
1142        Dynamic Address. This Dynamic Address transfer procedure shall have the following steps:
1143         • The Main Master shall drive the 7-bit Dynamic Address, followed by a parity bit (PAR), which
1144            is calculated as odd parity. Odd parity is the inverse of the XOR of the 7 bits. Therefore
1145            ~XOR( dynamic_address [7:1] ) is placed in position 0.
1146         • If the parity is valid, then the Slave shall acknowledge receipt of the Dynamic Address on the
1147            next SCL clock. If the parity is invalid, then the Slave shall passively NACK on the next SCL.


                     I3C Reserved Byte                I3C Modal Broadcast
                 S                          ACK                                   T
                      (7'h7E) (RnW=0)                   CCC (ENTDAA)




                     I3C Reserved Byte                    Read Data: 8 Bytes      Assign 7-bit Dynamic         ACK/
                Sr                          ACK                                                          PAR
                      (7'h7E) (RnW=1)                (48-bit Unique ID, BCR, DCR) Address to the Slave         NACK




                     I3C Reserved Byte                    Read Data: 8 Bytes      Assign 7-bit Dynamic         ACK/
                Sr                          ACK                                                        PAR
                      (7'h7E) (RnW=1)                (48-bit Unique ID, BCR, DCR) Address to the Slave         NACK




                     I3C Reserved Byte

                      (7'h7E) (RnW=1)




                     From Master to Slave                  Transition Bit             ACK = Acknowledge (SDA Low)
                     (Push-Pull and/or Open Drain)         (Parity Bit for CCC)       NACK = Not Acknowledge (NACK)
                                                                                      S    = START Condition
