                     (Open-Drain)                          (without Handoff)
1148
1149                            Figure 19 Dynamic Address Assignment Transaction

1150   10. The Main Master shall repeat this procedure, jumping back to step 4 until there is no ACK from
1151       any Device present on the I3C Bus.
1152   11. This Dynamic Address Assignment procedure shall be ended by the Main Master issuing a STOP.
1153   Regarding this Dynamic Address Assignment procedure, note that:
1154    • The Main Master is able to end the Dynamic Address Assignment procedure at any time, even if
1155      some of the pre-established I3C Devices have not yet received their Dynamic Addresses.
1156    • The Dynamic Address Assignment procedure can be started again any time the I3C Bus is in Bus
1157      Available Condition, using the Command Code Enter Dynamic Address Assignment (ENTDAA)
1158      (see Section 5.1.9.3.4).
1159    • If a given Slave does not acknowledge its assigned Dynamic Address, then the procedure requires
1160      the Main Master to continue from step 4. The Slave will then participate in the Address Arbitration
1161      using the same 48-bit Provisional ID, and as a result the Slave will win the Arbitration round. If the
1162      Slave does not ACK the Dynamic Address a second time, then the Main Master shall exit the
1163      Dynamic Address Assignment procedure and execute an error management procedure provided by
1164      the I3C Bus designer.

       52                                    Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                        All rights reserved.
                                                           Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                       Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

1165    • Any Secondary Masters wishing to receive the Dynamic Addresses assigned to all Devices present
1166      on the I3C Bus can do one, or both, of the following:
1167       • Follow the Dynamic Address Allocation procedure. A Secondary Master that follows the same
1168          procedure will have received all the same data.
1169       • Use the Command Code Define List of Slaves (DEFSLVS) (see Section 5.1.9.3.7) to acquire
1170          (eventually selectively) the Addresses and Characteristics of all Devices on the I3C Bus from
1171          the Main Master.
1172   After the Dynamic Address Assignment process is complete, the Main Master knows which Device on the
1173   I3C Bus is the Secondary Master. The Main Master then addresses the Secondary Masters with the Command
1174   Code Define List of Slaves (DEFSLVS) (see Section 5.1.9.3.7) and transfers the data for any and all Legacy
1175   I2C Devices on the I3C Bus. In order to keep the I3C Bus under control, this could all be done within the
1176   same transaction (i.e. the Main Master continues the communication by issuing a Repeated START each
1177   time, with no intervening STOP).

       5.1.4.3         Provisional ID Collision Detection and Correction
1178   In the Dynamic Address Assignment procedure described in Section 5.1.4.2, multiple I3C compliant Devices
1179   will receive the same Dynamic Address if they provide the Main Master with both identical 48-bit Provisional
1180   IDs, and identical Device Characteristic Register values. Although the probability of such a coincidence is
1181   quite small the potential does exist, and the I3C Bus will not operate properly under such conditions (at least
1182   the Instance IDs must be different, see Section 5.1.4.1.1). As a result the Main Master must test for this
1183   collision condition, and resolve it if necessary, before the I3C Devices present on the I3C Bus can all be
1184   safely used together.
1185   The I3C Main Master knows the number of Devices resident on the I3C Bus requiring Dynamic Address
1186   Assignment (per procedure step 1.a. in Section 5.1.4.2), which enables detection of collision errors. At
1187   completion of the Dynamic Address Assignment procedure the Main Master shall compare this expected
1188   number to the final count of Static Addresses and Dynamic Addresses that were actually assigned. If fewer
1189   Dynamic Addresses were assigned than expected, then multiple Devices must have received the same
1190   Dynamic Address, i.e. a collision has occurred.
1191   If this collision condition is detected, then the Main Master shall resolve it using either of the following
1192   methods:
1193     • The Main Master directs a Reset Dynamic Address request to the Dynamic Address most likely to
1194        have been duplicated, using the Command Code Reset Dynamic Address Assignment
1195        (RSTDAA) (see Section 5.1.9.3.3).
1196     • Alternatively, the Main Master resets the Dynamic Address Assignment process by using the
1197        Broadcast Command Code Reset Dynamic Address Assignment (RSTDAA) (per
1198        Section 5.1.9.3.3), and then proceeding from step 3 of the procedure in Section 5.1.4.2 from step 8,
1199        until the sooner of either:
1200         a. The expected number of I3C Devices is discovered, or
1201         b. A set maximum number of attempts fail. If this limit is reached, then a system error message
1202              shall be sent to the Host. To avoid freezing the entire system, a limit of three (3) attempts is
1203              recommended.




                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                   53
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                       Version 1.0
                                                                                                         19-Jul-2018

       5.1.5      Hot-Join Mechanism
1204   The I3C protocol supports a Hot-Join mechanism, to allow Slaves to join the I3C Bus after it is already
1205   configured.
1206   Note:
1207        Hot-Join does not allow Slaves to join the I3C Bus before the I3C Bus has been configured.
1208   Hot-Join may be used for:
1209    • I3C Devices mounted on the same board, but de-powered until needed. Such Devices shall not
1210      violate electrical limits for Slaves when depowered (or while transitioning) as defined in Section 6,
1211      including capacitive load.
1212    • I3C Devices mounted on a module/board that is physically inserted after the I3C Bus has already
1213      been configured. This specification does not attempt to address how that physical insertion is
1214      handled, however such insertion shall not disrupt the SCL and SDA lines, including respecting all
1215      electrical limits defined in Section 6.
1216   Hot-Joining Slaves may be any valid Slave type, including Secondary Master. After a Hot-Join, the Current
1217   Master shall use the DEFSLVS CCC as described in Section 5.1.9.3.7. To ensure that any Secondary Masters
1218   are aware of all of the available Slaves, the Master shall immediately notify the I3C Bus if it discovers that
1219   any previously joined Slaves are no longer present on the Bus (e.g. due either to non-response, or to
1220   mechanisms outside of the Bus).
1221   Since the Master is not inherently aware when new Slaves join the I3C Bus, the Hot-Join mechanism provides
1222   the following procedure for informing the Master about new Slaves:
1223   1. The Slave shall wait for at least tIDLE of Bus Available Condition (see Table 58).
1224   2. After the Slave determines that the Bus is Idle it may either issue a START, or else wait for a
1225        START. (To issue a START, pull the SDA line Low and hold it Low until the SCL line goes Low.)
1226   Note:
1227        The Hot-Join Device may power-up at the same time as the Main Master (e.g. may be connected to
1228        the I3C Bus when power is applied to the system). In that case, the Main Master might pull SDA Low
1229        even before the I3C Bus has been started, assuming that SCL and SDA are being pulled up. If a
1230        Main Master needs 1 ms or more in order to start acting on the I3C Bus, then that Main Master shall
1231        be able to accommodate the situation of SDA being held Low when it is ready to initialize the I3C
1232        Bus, or it may instead delay pulling SDA High, and/or pulling SCL High, until it is ready.
1233        Hot-Join capable Devices should implement some method that allows the system designer to
1234        prevent the Device from attempting to Hot-Join when the Device is not being used as a Hot-Join
1235        Device. For example: use of NVMEM, or else a pin strap could be used when the Device is
1236        soldered onto the board and powered with the rest of the I3C Bus.
1237   3.   The Slave issues the Reserved Slave Address of 7’b0000_010 as an IBI, using W (write) after the
1238        START. This requests a Dynamic Address Assignment process.
1239   4.   The Current Master shall perform one of 3 actions:
1240        a. NACK the request. The Slave shall try again at the next START.
1241        b. ACK the request, but issue a Broadcast CCC to disable Hot-Join by setting the DISHJ bit in
1242             the Command Code Enable/Disable Slave Events Command (ENEC/DISEC) (see
1243             Section 5.1.9.3.1). If another Device attempts to Hot-Join before the Master is ready to assign
1244             Dynamic Addresses to the Slaves, then the Master might need to repeat this procedure.
1245        c. ACK the request and then issue a Broadcast Command Code Enter Dynamic Address
1246             Assignment (ENTDAA) (see Section 5.1.9.3.4) to start the Dynamic Address Assignment
1247             process. Since only Slaves with no Dynamic Address shall participate, this allows the newly
1248             joined Slave to receive its Dynamic Address.
1249   5.   If an Enter Dynamic Address Assignment (ENTDAA) Command Code is issued as described in
1250        Section 5.1.9.3.4, then the Slave shall transmit its 48-bit Address as per the normal mechanism.

       54                              Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                       Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

1251       Hot-Join Slaves required to receive the same assigned Dynamic Address every time they join can
1252       use a fixed-value ID, per Section 5.1.4.1.1.
1253   If a Slave drops off the I3C Bus due to being detached or depowered, then the Master might not be aware of
1254   this. The Master may determine this condition by repeated attempts to contact the Slave using a safe (shall
1255   respond) Command Code such as Get Device Status (GETSTATUS) (see Section 5.1.9.3.15). If the Master
1256   determines that the Slave is no longer part of the I3C Bus, then it shall either recycle that Slave’s Dynamic
1257   Address, or else reserve that Slave’s Dynamic Address in case that Slave later re-joins the Bus.

       5.1.5.1         Failsafe Device Pads
1258   Hot-Join Devices shall be Failsafe, unless there is a protection method outside of the pad. Failsafe means that
1259   when the SCL and SDA pads are unpowered but wire connected to the I3C Bus, they must not draw extra
1260   leakage current from the Bus.
1261   The Device SCL and SDA pads shall be designed so as to avoid drawing current from the system SCL and
1262   SDA lines, both when they are being held High and when they are being held Low. Such leakage may be
1263   avoided by using special connectors, or other isolation methods (e.g., physical connection order in a plug).
1264   This excess current should be avoided whether due to diode effect, rail tie for ESD, or clamps. The leakage
1265   current variation between unpowered and powered shall not exceed the normal variation range of an active
1266   pad, Ii, as specified in Section 6, Table 54.




                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                   55
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                         Version 1.0
                                                                                                          19-Jul-2018

       5.1.6       In-Band Interrupt
1267   This Section specifies I3C’s priority-based In-Band Interrupt mechanism.

       5.1.6.1         Priority Level
1268   In I3C, Priority Level controls the order in which Slaves’ In-Band Interrupt requests and Master requests are
1269   processed. The Priority Level of each Slave in an I3C Bus instantiation is encoded in its Slave Address, with
1270   lower Addresses having higher Priority. That is, Slaves with lower value Addresses and higher Priority Levels
1271   have their In-Band Interrupts and Master requests processed sooner than Slaves with higher value Addresses
1272   and lower Priority Levels. This Priority Level ordering is a natural outcome of the I3C Address Arbitration
1273   behavior specified at Section 5.1.2.2.1, where Address bits with value 0 prevail bits with value 1.
1274   As a result, during each Dynamic Address Assignment operation (see Section 5.1.4) the Main Master should
1275   assign lower Addresses to Slaves for higher Priority In-Band Interrupt requests.

       5.1.6.2         I3C Slave Interrupt Request
1276   In order to request an interrupt, an I3C Slave shall emit its Address into the arbitrated Address header
1277   following a START (but not following a Repeated START). If no START is forthcoming within the Bus
1278   Available Condition, then the I3C Slave may issue a START Condition by pulling the SDA line Low, and
1279   then wait for the Current Master to pull the SCL Low.
1280   If the Current Master sets the SCL line Low, thus completing the START Condition, and then provides clocks
1281   on the SCL line, then the Slave shall drive the SDA line with its own Address, followed by an RnW bit with
1282   value 1’b1. If more than one Slave has issued an IBI request after the same START Condition, then the
1283   Current Master shall process those IBIs in Priority Level order, per Section 5.1.6.1. The Slave(s) that lost the
1284   arbitration may issue another IBI request, but shall not do so until after the Bus Available Condition.
1285   At that point, the Current Master shall perform one of the following three actions:
1286   1. Accept the IBI by providing the ACK bit. The actions available to the Current Master depend upon
1287        the value of the Slave’s BCR [2] bit (in the Slave’s BCR register):
1288        a. If the I3C Slave’s BCR [2] bit is set to 1, then, per Section 5.1.1.2.1, the Current Master shall
1289             read the Mandatory Data Byte that follows the accepted IBI request at any “read” clock speed
1290             allowable by the Slave. This operation is similar to a “read” from the Slave and all the related
1291             rules apply. Note that the Current Master cannot avoid receiving the Mandatory Data Byte,
1292             since it is transmitted in Push-Pull mode.
1293             After reading the Mandatory Data Byte, the Current Master may take any other valid I3C
1294             action. For example, the Current Master could issue a STOP, or issue a Repeated START,
1295             or it could continuing reading additional Data Bytes from the Slave (if, for example, a
1296             private contract between the two Devices has been established in advance).
1297             If the Current Master completes this transfer before all additional Data Bytes are read,
1298             then the Slave shall not repeat the as yet unserviced IBI request; the Current Master has
1299             assessed the request, and will service it at a later time. Depending upon the character of
1300             the data, the Master can either:
1301             i. Attempt to read either the full data (e.g. for short data packets), or
1302             ii. Continue from the position at which the transfer was interrupted (e.g. for FIFO transfers),
1303                  or
1304             iii. Abort the IBI service (e.g. for Timing Control information).
1305             In all cases the specific follow-up actions shall be established by private contract between
1306             the devices, bearing in mind the possibility for data to be corrupted in the meantime.
1307             One conceptual time diagram of this sequence is shown in Figure 20 below:




       56                               Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                   All rights reserved.
                                                      Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                        Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

                                                                                         Drive High or
        Open Drain         Open Drain           Open Drain     Hand Off    Push-Pull       Low, and       Push-Pull
                                                                                          then High-Z
                 S     Slave_addr_as_IBI/R     Master_ACK SCL High Slave_byte                  T              Sr

1308                            Figure 20 IBI Sequence with Mandatory Data Byte

1309        b.  If the I3C Slave’s BCR [2] bit is set to 0, then the Current Master may take any other valid
1310            I3C action. For example, the Current Master could issue a STOP, or issue a Repeated START,
1311            or it could continue reading additional Data Bytes from the Slave at any allowable “read”
1312            clock speed (if, for example, a private contract between the two Devices has been established
1313            in advance).
1314   2.   Refuse the IBI without disabling interrupts. To do this, the Current Master simply passively
1315        NACKs to deny the IBI. The Slave shall try again after the Bus Available Condition.
1316   3.   Refuse the IBI and disable interrupts. To do this, the Current Master NACKs to deny the IBI, then
1317        sends a Repeated START, and finally sets the DISINT bit in the Command Code Disable Slave
1318        Events Command (DISEC) to the interrupting Slave as described in Section 5.1.9.3.1. (The
1319        Current Master can set the ENINT bit in the Command Code Enable Slave Events Command
1320        (DISEC) at a later time.)
1321   In cases where the Main Master anticipates that the I3C Bus might be needed for a higher Priority Level
1322   transaction before the newly requested transaction would complete, the Master could either deny the access
1323   (by not acknowledging the request), or else drive the higher-priority Device Address instead of the Address
1324   of the Device sending the IBI request. The Main Master would then hold the SCL line Low until the expected
1325   higher-priority transaction begins.
1326   Bus contention occurs during Address evaluation. As a result, if multiple I3C Devices simultaneously attempt
1327   to win the Bus, then all but one will lose the Arbitration. These Devices will have the opportunity, but are not
1328   required, to repeat the attempt upon the next Bus Available Condition. Note that Slave Devices have the
1329   option to choose to not try again.

       5.1.6.3         I3C Secondary Master Requests to be Current Master
1330   When the I3C Secondary Master requests to be a Current Master:
1331   1. To perform the request, the I3C Secondary Master shall wait for either a START (not a Repeated
1332      START), or a Bus Available Condition and issue its own START.
1333   2. After a START, the I3C Secondary Master shall issue its own Dynamic Address on the I3C Bus,
1334      followed by the RnW bit of 0 to request to become Current Master.
1335   3. If the I3C Secondary Master wins the Arbitration by having the lower Dynamic Address, and if the
1336      Current Master is currently willing to relinquish Mastership to the requester, then the Current
1337      Master shall respond with ACK.
1338   4. After the ACK, the Current Master may issue one or more commands, and shall then issue a
1339      GETACCMST CCC (see Section 5.1.9.3.16) followed by a STOP, whereupon it releases control
1340      of the SCL line, and therefore also releases Master control of the I3C Bus.
1341   5. Following the STOP and Bus Available Condition, the I3C Secondary Master assumes the role of
1342      the Current Master and takes control of the I3C Bus.
1343   Figure 44 presents a simplified and generalized picture of the Master to Master Handoff procedure. While
1344   there are many possible variations to this process, the most significant states are as follows:
1345   1. At the end of data transfer, the Current Master (indicated by ‘CM’ in the Figure) controls SCL and
1346        SDA, and the New Master (‘NM’ in the Figure) has SCL and SDA in High-Z state.
1347   2. The Current Master provides the rising edge of SCL, while keeping SDA Low



                                        Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                   57
                                                   All rights reserved.
                                                      Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                         Version 1.0
                                                                                                           19-Jul-2018

1348   3.   After tSU_STO elapses, the Current Master drives SDA High using either an active drive, or the
1349        Open-Drain class Pull-Up. However, once SDA is High the Open Drain class Pull-Up shall be
1350        used.
1351   4.   After the New Master determines that SDA is High, and after both its Clock to Data Turnaround
1352        time and the time of flight elapse, it then takes two actions:
1353        a. The New Master Actively drives SCL High, overlapping with the Current Master’s active
1354            drive; and
1355        b. The New Master enables its Open-Drain class Pull-Up, in parallel with the Current Master’s
1356            Open-Drain class Pull-Up
1357        Neither of these actions conflicts with the Current Master.
1358        Although tSCO generally applies to a Slave Device, in this step it applies to the New Master
1359        because prior to the Bus transfer the New Master performs in the Slave role.
1360        In the Figure, the tSCO is shown starting as if the CM has used the Open-Drain drive of SDA High;
1361        this depicts the worst condition for this stage of the handoff, showing the latest possible moment
1362        when the NM starts overlapping the line controls with the CM.
1363   5.   After a time delay of tMMoverlap, the Current Master:
1364        a. Releases SCL to High-Z; and
1365        b. Disables its Open-Drain class Pull-Up on SDA, and sets SDA to High-Z
1366        In the Figure, tMMoverlap is shown in the worst case: on the Open-Drain drive of SDA, and where
1367        the CM starts counting it from the lower side of the SDA rising edge. The CM must control the
1368        lines until the NM could safely take over, and that is certain after tDIG_OD_L Min. As a result,
1369        tMMoverlap shall be greater than or equal to tDIG_OD_L Min.
1370   6.   After tMMlock (the time interval during which the New Master shall not drive SDA Low), the New
1371        Master could actively drive SDA Low, producing a START condition.
1372        The Slaves may also drive SDA Low at this point. This is allowed because SDA is held by an
1373        Open-Drain class Pull-Up. The tMMlock period timing parameter is similar to I3C’s tAVAL and I2C’s
1374        tBUF and, as such, requires different values for Pure Bus and for Mixed Bus, respectively.
1375        In the Figure, tMMlock is shown as referred to the SDA rising edge in Open-Drain driving mode,
1376        similar to I3C’s tAVAL and I2C’s tBUF.
1377   7.   After tCAS expires, the New Master may drive SCL Low, producing the first SCL falling edge.
1378   Following this SCL falling edge, the New Master shall actively drive SCL, while also driving SDA in Open-
1379   Drain mode with the arbitrable address.
1380   Note:
1381        The way the I3C Secondary Master indicates that it is a Secondary Master is by properly returning
1382        the value 2’b01 for Device Role in its BCR (see Section 5.1.1.2.1).
1383        The I3C Secondary Master records the Dynamic Addresses of all Devices on the I3C Bus. The
1384        preferred method is to record the Address from the Command Code Define List of Slaves
1385        (DEFSLVS) (see Section 5.1.9.3.7).
1386        The Master determines that this is a Current Master request by returning the value 2’b01 for Device
1387        Role in its BCR (see Section 5.1.1.2.1).




       58                              Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                       Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

       5.1.6.4         I3C Main Master Initiating a Transaction
1388   When initiating an I3C transaction, an I3C Current Master shall perform Dynamic Address Arbitration during
1389   the Address call. Any other I3C Device attempting to interrupt shall win the Arbitration. Interrupting Devices
1390   with lower-priority level shall wait for the next Bus Available Condition.
1391   Note:
1392       In order to allow the lower-priority Slaves to perform an In-Band Interrupt, Masters may do the
1393       following:
1394       1) Wait more than the minimum “Bus Available” period before starting a new communication.
1395       2) Transmit the reserved I3C Broadcast Address, followed by a Repeated START and normal
1396            Messages. (The reserved I3C Broadcast Address has lower priority than any interrupts.)




                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                  59
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                         Version 1.0
                                                                                                          19-Jul-2018

       5.1.7      Secondary Master Functions
1397   Once granted control of the Bus, the Secondary Master maintains control until another Master is granted Bus
1398   control. After the Secondary Master transitions to the Current Master role it could encounter Bus management
1399   activities besides the data transfers that it itself initiates. Some examples are the In-Band Interrupt, or the
1400   Hot-Join request. One optional possibility, shown at Section 5.1.7.2, is that the Secondary Master performs
1401   the Current Master’s actions with the full capabilities of the Main Master. Another optional possibility is that
1402   the Secondary Master, while serving in the Current Master role, could defer some actions to a more capable
1403   Master, as described in Section 5.1.7.3.
1404   A Secondary Master shall support the following scenarios according to its capabilities.

       5.1.7.1         Hardware and Software Requirements
1405   The Secondary Master capable of performing the Main Master tasks shall have the following minimum
1406   hardware and software capabilities:
1407   1. Memory for:
1408       a. All Bus Devices’ capabilities and functions at system level
1409       b. Retaining the Dynamic Addresses of all I3C Bus Devices
1410       c. Retaining the data transfer protocol that the I3C Bus Devices are capable of
1411   2. Link requirements for Slaves that are intended to transmit In-Band Interrupt requests (IBI), e.g. the
1412       clock speed and the maximum length of data transfer
1413   3. Data transfer protocol capability needed for communicating to all I3C Bus Devices

       5.1.7.2         Bus Management Procedures
1414   The Secondary Master capable of performing the Main Master tasks shall perform the following minimum
1415   Bus Management procedures:
1416   1. During Bus initialization, the Secondary Master shall obtain Characteristics of Devices on the Bus
1417       by either using the Command Code Define List of Slaves (DEFSLVS) (see Section 5.1.9.3.7) sent
1418       by the Main Master, or by monitoring the Bus during Dynamic Address assignment.
1419   2. Perform the Dynamic Address Assignment for Hot-Join Devices
1420       a. The Secondary Master needs to know all Bus Devices’ functionality at system level, and such
1421            in order to be able to assign the correct priority level to the Hot-Join Device
1422       b. The Current Master shall issue the Command Code Define List of Slaves (DEFSLVS) (see
1423            Section 5.1.9.3.7) to ensure that other Masters have the same knowledge
1424   3. Manage the In-Band Interrupt procedure
1425        The Secondary Master needs to know the meaning of the interrupt from the Slave, and needs to
1426        service the interrupt appropriately.
1427   4.   Procedure for requesting transfer of Bus control to another Master, including the Main Master
1428        The Secondary Master uses the Command Code Get Accept Mastership (GETACCMST) (see
1429        Section 5.1.9.3.16).




       60                               Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                   All rights reserved.
                                                      Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                     Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

       5.1.7.3        Reduced Functionality Secondary Masters
1430   Secondary Masters are not required to implement and be able to perform all the tasks required of the Main
1431   Master. The Secondary Master may defer or transfer the Bus control to a Master when needed; see
1432   Section 5.1.7.1 and Section 5.1.7.2.
1433   A Secondary Master with reduced functionality shall have the following minimum capabilities:
1434   1. Memory sufficient for retaining Device Addresses and Characteristics of any Slaves it supports
1435   2. Memory sufficient for retaining the Device Address and Characteristics of the Master to which it
1436       will return Bus control

       5.1.7.4        In-Band Interrupt Handling
1437   The Secondary Master can either service the In-Band Interrupt request as described in Section 5.1.6, or else
1438   defer In-Band Interrupt handling to a capable Master by setting the DISINT bit in the Command Code
1439   Disable Slave Events Command (DISEC) to the interrupting Slave (see Section 5.1.9.3.1).

       5.1.7.5        Hot-Join Management
1440   The Secondary Master can either manage the Hot-Join event as described in Section 5.1.5, or else defer
1441   management to a capable Master by issuing a Broadcast Command Code Disable Slave Events Command
1442   (DISEC) to disable Hot-Join by setting the DISHJ bit (see Section 5.1.9.3.1).




                                      Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                 61
                                                 All rights reserved.
                                                    Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                        Version 1.0
                                                                                                         19-Jul-2018

       5.1.8      Timing Control
1443   This section is not included in the I3C Basic Specification. To gain access to this capability, please contact
1444   MIPI Alliance.
1445   I3C includes optional Timing Control and Timestamping of events generated by I3C Devices resident on the
1446   I3C Bus. This Timing Control framework allows uncertainties affecting the transmission or reception of
1447   timing information (for example: Bus activity, busy Master or Slave, operation latency, system jitter, etc.) to
1448   be nullified.
1449   The I3C Timing Control framework provides:
1450    • Flexible implementation with significant capability enhancements
1451    • Means for synchronizing the timing references/clocks, and subsequently the events, of Slave
1452      Devices on the I3C Bus to the timing reference/clock of the Master
1453          This can result in reduced energy consumption at the system level.
1454    • Transmission of timing information, with minimal complexity for the Slave Devices
1455    • Controllable timing accuracy, suitable for the target use cases
1456   I3C defines two forms of systems and events for Timing Control. Only one form can be used on the I3C Bus
1457   at any time:
1458     • Synchronous Time Control (see Section 5.1.8.2): The Master emits a periodic time sync, which
1459       allows all Slaves to set their sampling time relative to this time sync. This avoids drift of individual
1460       Slaves’ clocks. It also ensures that samples occur close together in time, permitting data collected
1461       at the same time to be fused. It also provides a calibration method, enabling more accurate
1462       sampling between the time syncs.
1463     • Asynchronous Time Control (see Section 5.1.8.3): Slaves timestamp the moment at which they
1464       acquire sampled data, permitting the Master to time-correlate samples received from multiple,
1465       different sensors in an easier and more accurate manner. Timestamping ensures that the Master
1466       always has the time at which sensor data acquisition occurred (subject to the timing granularity
1467       supported), even if there are latencies in moving the data from the Slave to the Master. Four
1468       different Asynchronous Time Control versions (‘Modes’) provide different methods for measuring
1469       time, and/or calibrating timing, with increasing accuracy.

       5.1.8.1         General Principles
1470   This section is not included in the I3C Basic Specification. To gain access to this capability, please contact
1471   MIPI Alliance.
1472   The exchange of timing information is based on agreements between the Master and Slaves.
1473   The elements of this agreement are:
1474   1. Two I3C Common Command Codes:
1475        • Set Exchange Timing Information (SETXTIME), see Section 5.1.9.3.20, and
1476        • Get Exchange Timing Information (GETXTIME), see Section 5.1.9.3.21.
1477        These CCCs:
1478         • Identify Bus events and markers (i.e., specific SDA edge and/or SCL edge),
1479         • Transfer timing or control information (e.g., ‘abort’ command),
1480         • Specify which timing control procedure is in effect, and
1481         • Query the Slave Device for Exchange Timing support details, such as which Timing Control
1482            Mode(s) are supported, the current Timing Control state, frequency, inaccuracy, etc.
1483   2.   One defined and Mode-specific marker is sent by the Master, and used to synchronize the Slaves
1484   3.   One data collection event is sent by the Slave. It includes the time information previously
1485        requested by the Master.


       62                              Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                         Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

       5.1.8.2          Synchronous Systems and Events
1486   This section is not included in the I3C Basic Specification. To gain access to this capability, please contact
1487   MIPI Alliance.
1488   In systems where multiple sensors (or other Slave Devices) provide periodically sampled data, it is
1489   advantageous to instruct the Slaves to be able to collect the data at essentially synchronized times, so that the
1490   Master can read several Slaves’ data in a single system awake period.
1491   In a typical system, different Slaves will sample their data at different, uncorrelated times. This is true even
1492   if all Slaves are set to the same sampling frequency, because Slave-to-Slave oscillator accuracy differences
1493   will cause drift over time. This method permits all Slave data sampling to occur very close together in time,
1494   so that Slaves can prepare and activate their individual sampling mechanisms, even if there are variances
1495   across their clocks.

       5.1.8.3          Asynchronous Systems and Events
1496   This section is not included in the I3C Basic Specification. To gain access to this capability, please contact
1497   MIPI Alliance.
1498   This Section defines four variations, called Asynchronous Timing Control Modes, on a common procedure
1499   for increasing the precision of an acquired time-of-occurrence of an event in a sensor or other I3C Slave
1500   connected to an I3C Master. The four Asynchronous Modes are presented in order of increasing accuracy
1501   and/or precision.
1502   The actual event itself may or may not be periodic, and the Master and Slave devices need not use the same
1503   time base clock (source, frequency or accuracy). The procedure can help address the large
1504   inaccuracies/offsets to the event’s acquired time-of-occurrence that IBI launch/acknowledge latencies can
1505   introduce.
1506                              Table 13 Asynchronous Timing Control Modes
                 Mode                                               Description
        Async Mode 0:            Simple for I3C Master and I3C Slave
        Asynchronous             Allows all I2C out-of-band interrupt usages to be replaced with I3C In-Band
        Basic Mode               Interrupt
        Async Mode 1:            Extension to Async Mode 0
        Asynchronous             If clock used by Slave’s counter drifts, or is not accurate enough, then the Master
        Advanced Mode            must send an aperiodic event to limit the running time of the Slave’s timing
                                 counter.
        Async Mode 2:            Minimizes the time for which the clock driving the Slave’s timer counter needs to
        Asynchronous             run. Devices can use burst clock sources, at the cost of Master overhead to
        High-Precision           measure time and correlate time scales. Time reference is falling SCL when
        Low-Power Mode           Master ACK’s IBI
        Async Mode 3:            Precision controlled trigger followed by precision time-stamp of detected event.
        Asynchronous             Time stamp similar in operation to Mode 2, except time reference is to sync
        High-Precision           signal preceding trigger.

       5.1.8.3.2       Async Mode 1: Asynchronous Advanced Mode

       5.1.9        Common Command Codes (CCC)
1515   Common Command Codes (CCCs) are globally supported commands that can be transmitted either directly
1516   to a specific I3C Slave Device, or to all I3C Slave Devices simultaneously. This Section specifies how CCCs
1517   are transmitted on the I3C Bus, how each CCC functions, and which CCCs I3C Devices are required to
1518   support.

       5.1.9.1         CCC Command Format
1519   The CCC Command Protocol is only formatted using I3C SDR, and always starts with the I3C Broadcast
1520   Address (7’h7E). That is, after a START or Repeated START, the Address of a CCC Command shall always
1521   be a 7’h7E and the RnW bit shall always be a Write.
1522   All I3C Slaves shall recognize both the 7’h7E Broadcast Address, and their own Dynamic Address once it
1523   has been assigned. The I3C Master shall issue a CCC Command both before and after I3C Dynamic
1524   Addresses are assigned.
1525   Note:
1526        Because the I2C Specification ([NXP01] Section 3.1.12) reserves the Address value 7’h7E, no
1527        Legacy I2C Slave will match the I3C Broadcast Address.
1528   There are four categories of CCC Command:
1529   1. Broadcast Write: A Broadcast Write CCC is seen by all I3C Slaves. All Slaves shall inspect every
1530       received Broadcast command, even if the Slave then ignores the Broadcast command.
1531             Every Broadcast Write CCC Command ends with a Repeated START or a STOP, except
1532             ENTDAA (see Section 5.1.9.3.4) which always ends with a STOP.
1533   Note:
1534        If the CCC Command enters a Mode, then the new Mode ends according to its own rules.
1535   2.   Direct Read/Write: A Direct Read/Write CCC may alternatively read or write data from/to one or
1536        more specific I3C Slaves, one Slave at a time, selected by the Slave Dynamic Address(es). For
1537        example, it may Write to, and then Read back from, the same Slave to verify that a change was
1538        accepted.
1539             Every Direct Read/Write CCC Command ends with a STOP or a Repeated START,
1540             followed by the I3C Broadcast Address (7’h7E).
1541        Note:
1542             This type may also have data both provided with the CCC (code), as well as with each write.
1543   3.   Direct Write: A Direct Write CCC is directed to one or more specific I3C Slaves, selected by the
1544        Slave Dynamic Address(es).
1545            Every Direct Write CCC Command ends with a STOP or a Repeated START, followed by
1546            the I3C Broadcast Address (7’h7E).
1547   4.   Direct Read: A Direct Read CCC reads data from one or more specific I3C Slaves, one Slave at a
1548        time, selected by the Slave Dynamic Address(es).
1549             Every Direct Read CCC Command ends with a STOP or a Repeated START, followed by
1550             the I3C Broadcast Address (7’h7E).
1551   All CCC Commands share the same general Frame format, which is shown in Figure 21 for Broadcast CCCs,
1552   and in Figure 22 for Direct CCCs. Each CCC Command has a unique Command Code. The fields within
1553   this Frame format are detailed in Table 14.




                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                65
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                                                                       Version 1.0
                                                                                                                                                        19-Jul-2018

                                                                                                            End of this Broadcast CCC

                                           S
                                                                            Defining
                                                             Broadcast                      Data                 P
                                                 7’h7E                       Byte
                                                               CCC                        (Optional)

       5.1.9.2        Broadcast CCCs vs Direct CCCs
1559   The Command Code space is divided into Broadcast Commands and Direct Commands:
1560        • Broadcast Commands are Command Codes 0x00 to 0x7F
1561        • Direct Commands are Command Codes from 0x80 to 0xFE
1562        • Command Code 0xFF is reserved.
1563   As a result, a Slave can easily determine whether a received Command is being Broadcast to all Slaves on
1564   the I3C Bus (1’b0), or is a Direct Command (1’b1) intended only for the particular Slave, by inspecting Bit
1565   7 (MSb) of the Command Code.

       5.1.9.2.1      End of a CCC Command
1566   A CCC Command shall end in one of the following three I3C Bus conditions:
1567    • A STOP after the Command or Data
1568    • For a Broadcast Command, a Repeated START (for any Address value)
1569    • For a Direct Command, a Repeated START followed by 7’h7E (which may be the start of a new
1570      CCC, or may be followed by a Repeated START)
1571   If the CCC Command enters a Mode, then the Mode ends according to its own rules.
1572   A 7’h7E following a Repeated START to end a Direct CCC may start another CCC, or may be followed by
1573   a Repeated START.
1574   Although not a normal use, the Master may terminate a Direct CCC Command without addressing any Slave.
1575   If the Master invalidly terminates the data associated with a CCC prematurely, then the Slave shall use best
1576   efforts to handle the termination and ascertain the proper course of action. That is, the Slave may choose to
1577   disregard the event with no effect, or the Slave may process a partial impact from the incomplete data.

       5.1.9.2.2      Framing Model for Direct CCC Commands
1578   In Direct CCC Commands the Frame contains first the Command Code, then one or more Repeated STARTs,
1579   then the Address of the targeted Slave Device, followed by Data, and finally either a STOP, or a Repeated
1580   START and 7’h7E.
1581   A single Direct CCC Command may also optionally address more than one Slave Device. To do this, one
1582   additional block is inserted before the final 7’h7E for each additional desired Slave Device (see Figure 22).
1583   Each such block consists of Repeated START, the Slave Address of the additional desired Slave Device, and
1584   the Data to be sent to that Slave Device.
1585   With Direct Read/Write Direct CCC Commands, the command code may be followed by data, such as a
1586   defining byte. This will be explained in the details for each CCC. Further, each Slave access may be a Write
1587   with 0 or more data bytes following from the Master, or it may be a Read with 1 or more data bytes following
1588   from the Slave, if it has ACKed the Read. Each Direct CCC will explain whether and how it uses the Write
1589   and Read.
1590   Each addressed Slave Device may either ACK or NACK the Direct CCC Command. For a Read request, the
1591   Slave Device then returns the requested data in accordance with the SDR Standard Read model.
1592   Slaves terminate the Read in I3C, and future Direct Get CCC definitions could be extended to include
1593   additional data bytes, beyond those specified in this version of the I3C Specification. As a result, all I3C
1594   Masters Devices shall ignore any additional, unrecognized data bytes (i.e. more data bytes than this
1595   Specification defines for the given CCC Command) that the Slave Device might return in response to a Direct
1596   Get CCC.




                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                 67
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                       Version 1.0
                                                                                                        19-Jul-2018

       5.1.9.2.3       Retry Model for Direct GET CCC Commands
1597   I3C mandates a single-retry model for Direct GET CCC Commands.
1598   Recall that a Direct GET CCC Command first sends an overall GET command to all Slave Devices using the
1599   Broadcast Address 7’h7E, and then sends targeted Slave Address(es) in order to stimulate per-Slave
1600   response(s). This requires the Slave to be in a state allowing an immediate response if its Address is
1601   transmitted, but also to be prepared for its Address not to be transmitted (and therefore for the Slave not to
1602   have to actually respond). For Direct GET CCC Commands that the Slave supports in hardware, such as
1603   those dealing with information locally known within the Slave (like GETBCR), this requirement generally
1604   presents no issues. But for Direct GET CCCs supported by the Slave's system, or those supported by software
1605   in the Slave, it’s possible that the Slave might not be able to respond to the Direct GET CCC in time.
1606   Such situations are handled with the following single-retry model, which applies to Direct GET CCC
1607   Commands only.
1608   If a Slave cannot provide a response to a Direct GET CCC Command in time, then:
1609   1. The Slave shall NACK its Address.
1610   2. The Master shall then emit a Repeated START, followed by the Slave Address. Note that this is a
1611        second transmission of the Slave Address. This gives the Slave extra time to prepare its response.
1612        As a further measure to give the Slave even more time to prepare its response, the Master may
1613        optionally also employ a clock delay (as per Section 5.1.2.5) after the Slave’s NACK and before
1614        the Master’s Repeated START.
1615   3. The Slave should respond to the retry attempt in step 2 with an ACK, and then transmit the results
1616        requested by the Direct GET CCC.
1617   This Retry Model is limited to a single retry. Any Slave still unable (for any reason) to respond to the retry
1618   attempt from step 2 will NACK it. If a Slave NACKs the second attempt in step 2, then the Master shall not
1619   attempt further retries to that Slave.
1620   Note that step 2 only occurs if the Slave NACKs the original Direct GET CCC request. As a result, this retry
1621   model never imposes a time penalty, except in cases where the Slave actually needs the extra time.

       5.1.9.3.1        Enable/Disable Slave Events Command (ENEC/DISEC)
1626   These four Direct (Table 16) or Broadcast (Table 17) CCCs allows the Master to control when Slave-initiated
1627   traffic is (Enable) vs. is not (Disable) allowed on the I3C Bus. This control governs a Slave’s attempts to
1628   request an Interrupt (ENINT/DISINT), to request Mastership (ENMR/DISMR), or to signify a Hot-Join event
1629   (ENHJ/DISHJ).
1630                                     Table 16 ENEC/DISEC Format 1: Direct
                                         Direct                                       En/Dis Slave     Sr 7’h7E
                   7’h7E                                         Slave Addr
        S                            ENEC/DISEC CCC       Sr                           Event Byte
                 / W / ACK                                        / W / ACK
                                           /T                                              /T              P

1631                                   Table 17 ENEC/DISEC Format 2: Broadcast

        S                               Broadcast                  Enable/Disable Slave               Sr 7’h7E
                   7’h7E
                                     ENEC/DISEC CCC                    Event Byte
                 / W / ACK
        Sr                                 /T                              /T                              P


1632   Table 18 and Table 19 show the bits to set in the Slave Event Byte to enable and disable, respectively, the
1633   four supported I3C Slave/Secondary Master event types. Reserved bits are for future MIPI use.
1634                            Table 18 Enable Slave Events Command Byte Format
              Bit 7          Bit 6       Bit 5        Bit 4      Bit 3        Bit 2       Bit 1        Bit 0
                                 Reserved                       ENHJ       Reserved       ENMR        ENINT

1635                           Table 19 Disable Slave Events Command Byte Format
              Bit 7          Bit 6       Bit 5        Bit 4      Bit 3        Bit 2       Bit 1        Bit 0
                                 Reserved                       DISHJ      Reserved      DISMR        DISINT

1636   The supported I3C Slave/Secondary Master event types are:
1637    • Slave Interrupt Requests: Enable (ENINT) / Disable (DISINT)
1638      These bits allow the Master to control when Slave-initiated Interrupts are (ENINT) and are not
1639      (DISINT) allowed on the I3C Bus.
1640    • Mastership Requests: Enable (ENMR) / Disable (DISMR)
1641      These bits allow the Current Master to control when Mastership requests from Secondary Masters
1642      are (ENMR) and are not (DISMR) allowed on the I3C Bus.
1643    • Hot-Join Event: Enable (ENHJ) / Disable (DISHJ)
1644         These bits allow the Master to control when Slave-initiated Hot-Join is (ENHJ) and is not (DISHJ)
1645         allowed on the I3C Bus. The Master can Broadcast this CCC in order to command Devices to
1646         refrain from making Dynamic Address Assignment requests until later authorized by the Master, in
1647         case the Master isn’t ready to service the Hot-Joining Device(s). (Hot-Join events are
1648         asynchronous.)

                                                    All rights reserved.

1652   per Activity State. All I3C Slave Devices shall maintain I3C communications capabilities in all Activity

       5.1.9.3.5        Set/Get Max Write Length (SETMWL/GETMWL)
1668   These Direct and Broadcast CCCs (Direct Set or Get in Table 26, Broadcast Set in Table 27) allow the I3C
1669   Master to Set or Get a maximum data write length in bytes for one Slave Device. This Max Write Length
1670   does not affect data write lengths for Broadcast CCCs. The Set/Get Max Write Length value is transmitted
1671   over two bytes, with the most significant byte (MSb) transmitted first. The minimum value that Max Write
1672   Length can be set to is 8.
1673   This CCC is required if (and only if) any private Write Message(s) and/or any extended Write CCC(s)
1674   implemented by the Slave Device support a variable limit on the maximum number of data bytes per
1675   Message, and this limit is greater than 8 bytes. This allows the Slave to limit the number of bytes the Master
1676   sends. A Slave Device with no such settable limit may optionally support this CCC, but is not expected to do
1677   so.
1678                                   Table 26 Direct SETMWL/GETMWL Format
                               SETMWL or                                                                Sr   7’h7E
                7’h7E                                     Slave Addr      MWL MSb        MWL LSb
        S                     GETMWL CCC            Sr
               / W / ACK                                  / RnW / ACK         /T             /T
                                       /T                                                                    P

1679                                        Table 27 Broadcast SETMWL Format

                7’h7E         SETMWL CCC                   MWL MSb                  MWL LSb             Sr   7’h7E
        S
               / W / ACK               /T                      /T                       /T

       5.1.9.3.6        Set/Get Max Read Length (SETMRL/GETMRL)
1680   These Direct and Broadcast CCCs (Direct Set or Get in Table 28, Broadcast Set in Table 29) allow the I3C
1681   Master to Set or Get a maximum data read length, and optionally a maximum IBI payload size.
1682   The Set/Get Max Read Length value is transmitted over the first two bytes, with most significant byte (MSb)
1683   transmitted first. The minimum value to which Max Read Length can be set is 16.
1684   For devices with BCR bit 2 set to 1’b1, the Max IBI payload size value is added as a third byte, where a value
1685   of 0 indicates an unlimited payload size. The minimum IBI payload size is one (one byte).
1686   This CCC is optional for the Slave, with two exceptions:
1687   1. This CCC is required if both (a) any private Read Request Message(s) and/or any extended Read
1688       Request CCC(s) implemented by the Slave support a variable limit on the maximum number of
1689       data bytes that the Slave may return per Message, and (b) this limit is greater than 16 bytes.
1690   2. This CCC is required if the Slave both (a) supports an IBI Payload (as indicated with BCR bit 1),
1691       and (b) will transmit more than one byte of private payload.
1692                                 Table 28 Direct SETMRL/GETMRL Format

       5.1.9.3.7       Define List of Slaves (DEFSLVS)
1694   This Broadcast CCC (Table 30) is only relevant to Secondary Masters, which may independently elect either
1695   to respond to this CCC, or to ignore it. This CCC tells Secondary Master Devices what Slaves are present on
1696   the I3C Bus, via four consecutive Data Bytes per Slave. First the Current Master identifies itself by
1697   transmitting its own data in the first set of four data bytes, using the value 7’h7E as the Static Address. Then
1698   each additional Slave on the I3C Bus Slave is represented by a further set of four data bytes.
1699                                           Table 30 DEFSLVS Format

                                                                               Describes First Slave
                                         Describes Current Master
                                                                          (Any additional Slaves will follow)

                                     Dynamic    DCR      BCR    Static   Dynamic DCR         BCR       Static
        S 7’h7E DEFSLVS                                                                                         Sr 7’h7E
                        Count         Addr      Type     Type   Addr      Addr   Type        Type      Addr
             /W   CCC
                         /T           Master   Master Master 7’h7E          0      0           0         0
           / ACK   /T
        Sr                             /T       /T     /T      /T          /T     /T          /T        /T         P

1700   Count is the number of Slaves present on the I3C Bus. Each Slave is represented by a set of four data bytes:
1701   1. Dynamic Address: The 7 most significant bits (Bits [7:1]) contain the current value of the Slave’s
1702      Main Master-assigned 7-bit Dynamic Address, and the least significant bit (Bit [0]) is filled with
1703      the value 1’b0. For Legacy I2C Devices, the value of the Dynamic Address shall be 7’h00.
1704   2. DCR Type: The Slave’s Device Characteristics Register value, or 0x00 if unknown. For Legacy
1705      I2C Devices the value of the DCR Type shall be the Device’s LVR value.
1706   3. BCR Type: The Slave’s Bus Characteristics Register value, or 0x00 if unknown.
1707   4. Static Address: The Slave’s original 7-bit static I2C Address in the 7 most significant bits
1708      (Bits [7:1]) with the least significant bit (Bit [0]) filled with the value 1’b0. If no Static Address,
1709      then the value shall be 7’h00.
1710   For a Legacy I2C Device, the value of the Dynamic Address field will be 7’h00, and the DCR field will
1711   contain the value of the Device’s Legacy Virtual Register (LVR).
1712   The Master shall not send the DEFSLVS CCC unless at least one Secondary Master Device is present on the
1713   I3C Bus. The Master shall send the DEFSLVS CCC following every “Hot-Join” event.

       5.1.9.3.10      Set Dynamic Address from Static Address (SETDASA)
1724   This CCC (Table 34 and Table 35 illustrate the two distinct command formats) allows the Master to assign a
1725   Dynamic Address to one Slave using the Slave’s Static Address. This is faster than the ENTDAA Dynamic
1726   Address Assignment procedure (see Section 5.1.9.3.4). The SETDASA CCC should be used before the
1727   ENTDAA CCC is used; all Slaves without assigned Dynamic Addresses will respond to the ENTDAA CCC.
1728   Note:
1729        The SETDASA Command Code is unusual in that it addresses the desired I3C Device by its I2C
1730        Static Address, rather than by its Dynamic Address. As a result, this CCC can only be sent to a Slave
1731        that has an I2C Static Address.
1732   The newly assigned Address is transmitted in the Dynamic Address Byte shown in Table 34, where the 7
1733   most significant bits (Bits [7:1]) contain the 7-bit Dynamic Address, and the least significant bit (Bit [0]) is
1734   filled with the value 1’b0.
1735                                   Table 34 SETDASA Format 1: Primary
                                                                                       7-bit              Sr 7’h7E
                  7’h7E        SETDASA CCC                  Slave Addr
        S                                         Sr                             Dynamic Address / 0
                / W / ACK              /T                     / W / ACK
                                                                                           /T                  P

       SETDASA Minimal Bus Point-to-Point Communication
1736   The SETDASA CCC may also be specially used for simple point-to-point communication in I3C Minimal
1737   Bus use cases. An I3C Minimal Bus is an I3C Bus with one I3C Master Device (potentially with reduced
1738   functionality), and one I3C Slave Device. In this special usage of the SETDASA CCC, the Master Device
1739   both uses the fixed value 7’h01 as the Static Address, and uses the fixed (and reserved) value of 7’h01 as the
1740   Dynamic Address (see Table 35). This special usage of the SETDASA CCC allows for simpler Master
1741   Devices, and optionally simpler Slave Devices intended for use specifically in Minimal Bus configurations.
1742   I3C Slave Devices should support this special usage of the SETDASA CCC unless such support would make
1743   the Slave Device unusable in a Minimal Bus use case. A Slave Device supporting this special Minimal Bus
1744   usage of the SETDASA CCC shall match the Static Address 7’h01, and then accept 7’h01 as its new Dynamic
1745   Address (same as the natural result of SETDASA). The Slave Device may choose to behave differently after
1746   receiving its Dynamic Address in this manner.
1747   An I3C Master shall not use this special form of the SETDASA CCC unless the I3C Bus is known to have
1748   precisely one Master and either:
1749     • One active I3C Slave Device that is capable of supporting this special usage of the SETDASA
1750       CCC, or
1751     • One or more I3C Slave Devices that act strictly as receivers, i.e., that do not use In-Band Interrupt
1752       and that will not be sent Read requests. This arrangement permits a single Master Device to, in
1753       effect, Broadcast to the Slave Devices.
1754                                Table 35 SETDASA Format 2: Point-to-Point

                  7’h7E         SETDASA CCC                    7’h01                   7’h01 / 0          Sr   7’h7E
        S                                         Sr
                / W / ACK              /T                    / W / ACK                    /T
                                                                                                               P

       5.1.9.3.16      Get Accept Mastership (GETACCMST)
1775   This Direct Get CCC (Table 42) is used both to verify a Master Request, and to allow the Current Master to
1776   offer Mastership to an I3C Secondary Master. The Get is used to confirm acceptance; to do so, the Secondary
1777   Master returns its 7-bit Dynamic Address as shown in Table 42. The value of the 7-bit Dynamic Address will
1778   be the same as the value of the Slave Address. If the Secondary Master does not accept, then it shall NACK
1779   the Get request as shown in Table 43.
1780   A Secondary Master requesting Mastership will gain Mastership only if all four of the following steps occur
1781   in the indicated order. For a Current Master offering Mastership, only steps 2 through 4 apply.
1782   1. The Current Master ACKs the Mastership request
1783   2. The Current Master transmits a GETACCMST command to the Secondary Master
1784   3. The Secondary Master correctly replies with its 7-bit Dynamic Address. The value of the 7-bit
1785        Dynamic Address will be the same as the value of the Slave Address.
1786            If the Secondary Master instead responds with NACK (Table 43), or with an incorrect 7-
1787            bit Address (Table 44), then:
1788              • The Secondary Master will not acquire Mastership
1789              • The GETACCMST command is canceled, and
1790              • The Current Master can then either (a) Retry the CCC, or (b) End the transaction by
1791                 providing a STOP.
1792   4.   The Current Master issues a STOP
1793            If the Current Master instead issues a Repeated START, then:
1794              • The Secondary Master will not gain Mastership
1795              • The GETACCMST command is canceled (Table 44), and
1796              • The Current Master can then either (a) Retry the CCC, or (b) End the transaction by
1797                 providing a STOP.
1798                                 Table 42 GETACCMST Format 1: Accepted
                                     GETACCMST                                      7-Bit Dynamic
                    7’h7E                                       Slave Addr
        S                               CCC              Sr                            Address                P
                 / W / ACK                                       / R / ACK
                                             /T                                          / PAR / T

1799   Note:
1800        The value of the 7-Bit Dynamic Address will be the same as the value of the Slave Addr.

1801                             Table 43 GETACCMST Format 2: Not Accepted

                                  GETACCMST                                                                  7’h7E
                  7’h7E                                                    Slave Addr                 Sr
        S                            CCC            Sr                                                      or Retry
                / W / ACK                                                  / R / NACK
                                        /T                                                                    P

                                                                                     Incorrect               7’h7E
                                  GETACCMST                                                           Sr
                 7’h7E                                        Slave Addr           7-Bit Dynamic            or Retry
        S                            CCC            Sr
                / W / ACK                                      / R / ACK              Address
                                       /T                                                                     P

1803   This Direct CCC (Table 45) is only relevant to Bridging Devices, i.e., I3C Devices that the Master knows in
1804   advance (not by inspecting BCR bits) to be Bridging Devices. An I3C Master shall only send this CCC to
1805   known Bridging Devices that accept it.

1807   Slave Addr is either the Slave’s original 7-bit static I2C Address, or else 7’h00 if the Slave had no such static
1808   I2C Address.
1809   Count is the number of Bridged “Slaves”.
1810   Each described Bridged “Slave” is represented by two fields:
1811    • Dynamic Address: The 7 most significant bits (Bits [7:1]) contain the current value of the Slave’s
1812      Main Master-assigned 7-bit Dynamic Address, and the least significant bit (Bit [0]) is filled with
1813      the value 1’b0. For Legacy I2C Devices, the value of the Dynamic Address shall be 7’h00.
1814    • ID: A 16-bit unambiguous identifier for the Bridged Device (two bytes)
1815   The meaning of the ID field is not defined by this Specification, and should be a contract between the Bridge
1816   and the Master (or any other entity that supplies such information to the Master). For example, the upper
1817   nibble could indicate the communication type (e.g. I2C, SPI, UART, LIN, etc.), and the lower 12 bits could
1818   be the port (as port number and Device selector).

       5.1.9.3.18      Get Max Data Speed (GETMXDS)
1819   The Master uses this Direct CCC (there are two formats: Table 46 and Table 47) to determine the SDR Mode
1820   data speed limitations of one Slave Device. The Master is required to use this CCC only when Bit [0] of the
1821   addressed Slave Device’s Bus Control Register (BCR) is set to 1’b1 (see Table 6). A Slave Device is required
1822   to support this CCC only when Bit [0] of its Bus Control Register (BCR) is set to 1’b1 (see Table 6).
1823   Bit [0] of the Bus Characteristics Register shall be set in any Slave Device with a Clock-to-Data turnaround
1824   time greater than 12 ns. Slave Devices within the tSCO delay range should not set the limitation bit (i.e., Bit [0])
1825   unless other limitations exist. But Devices should support this CCC to allow better factoring of each number
1826   when computing the maximum effective frequency for reads, since the Master/System-designer can be told
1827   the tSCO parameter along with line length (propagation time), line capacitance, number of Slaves, and stubs
1828   if present.
1829   The tSCO delay is the measurement of the total internal delay from the SCL input to the SDA output, excluding
1830   other factors like line capacitance and path delay that are factored out of the device computation and put into
1831   the broader computation. The tSCO delay is applicable to both Master and Slave Devices, since Master Devices
1832   may behave as Slave device in a multi-Master system based on the system configuration.




1833
1834                    Figure 23 Components of Clock-to-Data Turnaround Delay (tSCO)

1835   Any Slave Devices with a Clock-to-Data turnaround (tSCO) delay greater than 12 ns shall set the Clock to
1836   Data Turnaround field of the maxRD Byte to 3’b111 and report this value to the Master by private agreement.
1837   Maximum Read Turnaround Time is used to notify the Master how long to wait before reading the data it
1838   requested. This allows the Master to get the Slave Device’s turnaround delay time for data read requests
1839   (excluding In-Band Interrupt responses). This is useful because some Slaves may exhibit data read delays (as
1840   contrasted with CCC reads) due to clock-starting effects, bridging, slower inner processors, etc. The Master
1841   can use the returned delay factor to avoid NACKs that would result from reading the Device too soon. The
1842   Slave may have the data ready before the time reported, and the Master can also estimate the time and retry
1843   reading before the maximum Read Turnaround time. The Master should be aware that any NACKs for read
1844   requests from the Slave before the maximum time is a normal condition, and no recovery methods are
1845   required; by contrast, NACKs after the maximum Data Turnaround time shall be dealt with accordingly.




       84                                Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                    All rights reserved.
                                                       Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                         Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

1846   Note:
1847        This CCC relates to bit data rates and Read Turnaround times, not data sizes. Data sizes are the
1848        subject of the CCCs Get Max Read Length (GETMRL, see Section 5.1.9.3.6) and Get Max Write
1849        Length (GETMWL, see Section 5.1.9.3.5).
1850   The Slave Device shall return either:
1851    • GETMXDS Format 1: Two data bytes containing the Slave Device’s Maximum Write Speed and
1852      Maximum Read Speed (see Table 46), or
1853    • GETMXDS Format 2: Five data bytes containing the Slave Device’s Maximum Write Speed,
1854      Maximum Read Speed, and a three-byte Maximum Read Turnaround Time (see Table 47).
1855   The Slave signals which Format it is returning via the number of returned data bytes: Two bytes indicates
1856   Format 1, and five bytes indicates Format 2.
1857   The Slave's selection of Format 1 vs. Format 2 should be based upon whether Maximum Read Turnaround
1858   Time needs to be communicated to the Master Device.
1859   Interpretation of the returned fields maxWr, maxRd, and (for Format 2 only) maxRdTurn is shown in Table
1860   48, Table 49, and Table 50 respectively.
1861                            Table 46 GETMXDS Format 1: Without Turnaround

                7’h7E                              Slave Addr     maxWr          maxRd      maxRdTurn
        S                      CCC            Sr

                         Data Rate for non-CCC         1: 8 MHz
                         messages sent by Slave        2: 6 MHz
                         Device to Master Device       3: 4 MHz
                                                       4: 2 MHz
                                                       5–7: Reserved for future use by the MIPI Alliance

1865                                         Table 50 maxRdTurn Format
             Byte                    Field                                    Description
               0         Least Significant Byte        Maximum Read Turnaround Time in µSeconds.
               1         Middle Byte
                                                       24-bit field can encode turnaround times from 0.0 seconds
               2         Most Significant Byte         to 16 seconds.

       5.1.9.3.19      Get HDR Capability (GETHDRCAP)
1866   This section is not included in the I3C Basic Specification. To gain access to this capability, please contact
1867   MIPI Alliance.

       5.1.9.3.20      Set Exchange Timing Information (SETXTIME)
1868   This section is not included in the I3C Basic Specification. To gain access to this capability, please contact
1869   MIPI Alliance.
1870   As detailed in Section 5.1.8, the SETXTIME CCC provides the framework for Master(s) and Slave(s) to
1871   exchange event timing information for purposes such as synchronizing controls, collecting or reconstructing
1872   timestamps, and specifying the timing data procedure. The SETXTIME CCC is available as both a Broadcast
1873   Command and as a Direct Command.

       5.1.9.3.21 Get Exchange Timing Support Information (GETXTIME)
1874   This section is not included in the I3C Basic Specification. To gain access to this capability, please contact
1875   MIPI Alliance.
1876   This Directed CCC, whose use is further detailed in Section 5.1.8, provides the framework for the Master to
1877   query the Exchange Timing capabilities supported by the I3C Slaves.

       5.1.10       Error Detection and Recovery Methods for SDR
1882   The error detection and recovery methods specified in this Section are provided in order to avoid fatal
1883   conditions when errors occur. A set of required methods is specified for I3C Slave Devices, and a separate
1884   set of required methods is specified for I3C Master Devices. Origins for all of these SDR Error Types are
1885   shown in Figure 53 through Figure 56.

       5.1.10.1          SDR Error Detection and Recovery Methods for I3C Slave Devices
1886   The seven Error Types summarized in Table 52 shall be supported for all I3C Slave Devices. Each Error Type
1887   is further explained below the table.
