       5.1.10.1.1      Error Type S0
1889   If an error occurs during Broadcast Address/W or Dynamic Address/RnW after a Dynamic Address is
1890   assigned, then the Slave will be unable to distinguish whether the transfer is a CCC transfer or a Private RnW
1891   transfer. For example, in the case of an ENTHDR CCC transfer the Slave is not able to know that the I3C
1892   Bus has changed to HDR Mode. A potentially fatal situation could ensue if this case is not detected and
1893   handled, because the Slave might become confused by seeing many STARTs and STOPs as illustrated in
1894   Figure 24, and might attempt to interpret the HDR transfer as though the I3C Bus were still in SDR Mode.

                STOP        START                      STOP          STOP                      START        START

        SDA

        SCL
1895
1896                              Figure 24 Example Waveform for Error Type S0

1897   In order to avoid this situation, the Master shall not use any of the possible error case Addresses: 7’h7F,
1898   7’h7C, 7’h7A, 7’h76, 7’h6E, 7’h5E and 7’h3E. Except during a Dynamic Address Arbitration procedure, the
1899   Slave shall consider receipt of any of these restricted Addresses, or the receipt of 7’h7E/R, as an error and
1900   shall then ignore the rest of the signal until the HDR EXIT pattern is detected.

       5.1.10.1.2      Error Type S1
1901   If the Slave detects a parity error during a CCC code, then the Slave will not be able to know that the I3C
1902   Bus has changed to HDR Mode if the CCC is ENTHDR. This is similar to the situation in Error Type S0. In
1903   order to avoid this situation, if the Slave detects a parity error during a CCC code, then the Slave shall ignore
1904   the rest of the signal, until the HDR EXIT pattern is detected.

       5.1.10.1.3      Error Type S2
1905   If the Slave detects a parity error during Write Data, then the Slave shall wait for STOP or Repeated START.
1906   If the Slave detects this error after receiving a CCC, then the Slave shall either:
1907   1. Retain the CCC state until the Slave detects the end of CCC command (i.e., when the Slave is
1908        recovered by the Repeated START condition), or else
1909   2. Stop the CCC when the Slave is recovered by the STOP condition.

       5.1.10.1.4      Error Type S3
1910   If the Slave detects a parity error in the PAR Bit of the Assigned Address during a Dynamic Address
1911   Arbitration procedure, then the Slave shall generate NACK (after PAR) and then wait for another Repeated
1912   START and 7E/R to re-transmit the Provisional ID.

       5.1.10.1.5      Error Type S4
1913   During a Dynamic Address Arbitration procedure, if the Slave detects any value other than 7’h7E/R following
1914   Repeated START, then the Slave shall generate NACK (after 7’h7E/R) and then wait for STOP to exit the
1915   ENTDAA mode.
1916   If the Slave detects this error after receiving a CCC, then the Slave shall either:
1917   1. Retain the CCC state until the Slave detects the end of CCC command (i.e., when the Slave is
1918        recovered by the Repeated START condition), or else
1919   2. Stop the CCC when the Slave is recovered by the STOP condition.



                                        Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                    89
                                                   All rights reserved.
                                                      Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                            Version 1.0
                                                                                                             19-Jul-2018

       5.1.10.1.6      Error Type S5
1920   If the Slave detects an illegally formatted CCC, then the Slave shall generate NACK (after the Dynamic
1921   Address) and then wait for STOP or Repeated START. An example of an illegally formatted CCC would be
1922   if the Slave receives the Dynamic Address/Write during the GETBCR CCC.
1923   If the Slave detects this error after receiving a CCC, then the Slave shall either:
1924   1. Retain the CCC state until the Slave detects the end of CCC command (i.e., when the Slave is
1925        recovered by the Repeated START condition), or else
1926   2. Stop the CCC when the Slave is recovered by the STOP condition.

       5.1.10.1.7      Error Type S6 (Optional)
1927   If an error occurs in a RnW Bit in a Private Read transfer, then the Write Data from the Master might conflict
1928   with the Read Data from the Slave.
1929   The Slave should always monitor the Data it transmits. If the Slave does so, then if the monitored Data differs
1930   from the Data the Slave intended to transmit (except for Data transferred during a Dynamic Address
1931   Arbitration procedure), the Slave shall consider that to be an error. If the Slave detects this error, it shall stop
1932   the transmission and then wait for STOP or Repeated START.
1933   If the Slave detects this error after receiving a CCC, then the Slave shall either:
1934   1. Retain the CCC state until the Slave detects the end of CCC command (i.e., when the Slave is
1935        recovered by the Repeated START condition), or else
1936   2. Stop the CCC when the Slave is recovered by the STOP condition.

       5.1.10.1.8      Optional Recovery Method for Error Types S0 and S1
1937   An I3C Slave can recover from an Error Type S0 or Error Type S1 situation not only by detecting the HDR
1938   EXIT pattern, but also by monitoring the SCL and SDA lines. If the Slave detects that both lines stay at High
1939   level for a period exceeding 60 µs, then the I3C Slave can regard the bus as operating in non-HDR mode.
1940   The I3C Slave can then recover from the S0 or S1 situation, and wait for a STOP or a START condition to
1941   resume normal operation.
1942   Note:
1943        Regarding timing, HDR’s slowest clock rate is 10 kHz (100 µs total cycle). The period 60 µs is derived
1944        by assuming that HDR (like DDR and TSP) will always keep an approximately even duty cycle,
1945        especially at very slow clock rates (since the only reason to go so slow is for long lines and/or large
1946        capacitive load on bus lines). 60 µs represents 60% of the duty cycle and therefore is a safe duration
1947        to wait when seeing both lines High.
1948        The Slave can start measuring the period whenever it detects that both SCL and SDA are at High
1949        level. There is no need to start timing this period at any particular signal pattern (for example, at the
1950        STOP condition).




       90                                Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                    All rights reserved.
                                                       Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                            Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

       5.1.10.2          SDR Error Detection and Recovery Methods for I3C Master Devices

1952   is further explained below the table.

       5.1.10.2.1        Error Type M0
1954   If the Master detects an illegally formatted CCC, then the Master shall stop the transmission, send STOP, and
1955   retry the transmission. An example of an illegally formatted CCC would be the Master receiving just one
1956   byte from the Slave in a GETMWL CCC code, since the Master expects two bytes.




                                        Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                         91
                                                   All rights reserved.
                                                      Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                          Version 1.0
                                                                                                           19-Jul-2018

       5.1.10.2.2      Error Type M1 (Optional)
1957   Note:
1958        Although this section describes methods to mitigate or handle the Type M1 error, this error is not an
1959        expected behavior.
1960   Error Type M1 occurs when the I3C Master detects that the transmitted data differs from what the Master
1961   intended to transmit. This might happen when the I3C Master or I3C Slaves misinterpret the RnW bit or the
1962   ACK/NACK during transactions (including IBI) defined by the I3C specification. This Section describes
1963   only two kinds of occurrence conditions for Error Type M1, and the recovery method for each kind. Note
1964   that Type M1 errors are not an expected behavior.
1965   If an error occurs in a RnW Bit in a Private Write transfer, then the Write Data from the Master might conflict
1966   with the Read Data from the Slave. For example, the Slave could misinterpret a Private Write transfer as a
1967   Read transfer if there is an error in the RnW Bit, and as a result Write Data from the Master would conflict
1968   with Read Data from the Slave.
1969   The Master should always monitor the Data it transmits. If the Master does so, then if the monitored Data
1970   differs from the Data the Master intended to transmit (except for Data transferred during a Dynamic Address
1971   Arbitration procedure), the Master shall consider that to be an error. If the Master detects this error, it shall
1972   stop the transmission, then send STOP and retry the transmission.
1973   If the Slave does not reply, then the Master shall transmit a pattern including HDR EXIT and STOP. This
1974   requirement applies regardless of whether the I3C Bus is in SDR Mode or in HDR Mode at the time.
1975   Example:
1976        The Master should try the following sequence of steps when attempting to recover the Slave:
1977        1. The Master transmits the Private Read several times.
1978             • If the Slave responds (including ACK), then the Slave temporarily did not have the Data
1979               needed for a response.
1980             • If there is no response from the Slave, then proceed to the next step.
1981        2. The Master transmits a pattern including HDR EXIT and STOP.
1982             • If the Slave responds (including ACK), then the Slave either detected an illegal format, or
1983               detected an error.
1984             • If there is no response from the Slave, then no Slave with that Address is present on the
1985               I3C Bus.

       5.1.10.2.3      Error Type M2
1986   If the Master does not receive an ACK of a transmitted Broadcast Address (7’h7E), then it shall transmit the
1987   HDR Exit Pattern followed by STOP in order to recover any Slave after S0, S1, S2, S5, and S6 errors.




       92                               Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                   All rights reserved.
                                                      Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                         Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

       5.1.10.2.4      Master Error Detection and Escalation Handling
1988   If the Master does not receive an ACK of a transmitted private Message to a Slave, and if the following
1989   conditions are all true:
1990     • Activity State is 0 (and has been)
1991     • Either the Slave Device has not indicated read-turnaround delays via GETMXDS, or the Slave
1992        Device has indicated a GETMXDS period and a period longer than GETMXDS has elapsed
1993     • The Slave Device has not notified the Master that it will be going into a lower Activity State via a
1994        private contract In-Band Interrupt (and either GETSTATUS or a private activity state status),
1995   then the Master has the following escalation options to recover the system:
1996   1. If the Master is aware that the Slave might sometimes need extra time, then the Master can choose
1997       to try again after a short delay.
1998   2. If that fails, then the Master shall check whether the Slave is responsive by issuing GETSTATUS
1999       to the Slave. The idea here is that the Slave might be forced to NACK a private Message due to its
2000       inner system being unready, whereas GETSTATUS is a lightweight request that does not
2001       necessarily involve the inner system.
2002   3. If that fails, then the Master shall use M2 error handling (see Section 5.1.10.2.3) to emit the
2003       sequence:
2004                             S | 7’h7E (W) | ACK/NACK* | HDR Exit Pattern | STOP
2005        Note:
2006           * The I3C Master doesn’t care whether the I3C Slaves’ response is ACK vs. NACK.
2007   4.   If that fails, and if the Slave is still not responsive, then the next step depends on the value of the
2008        BCR “Offline-Capable” bit (bit [3]):
2009        a. If the Slave is not Offline-Capable, then:
2010             i. The Master can try slowing the SCL clock rate, or try slowing its effective rate by using
2011                   duty cycle.
2012             ii. If that fails, then any further escalation is outside the scope of the I3C Specification.
2013        b. If the Slave is Offline-capable, then:
2014             i. If the Slave is known (i.e., via a private contract) to have a long wake period, and also
2015                   known to monitor the I3C Bus during that wake period, then the Master simply delays
2016                   and tries later, after a delay based on the known or expected wake up time. Escalation and
2017                   the GETSTATUS ensure that the Slave’s Dynamic Address was issued; that should serve
2018                   as the wake trigger. Either the Slave may issue an In-Band Interrupt at a later time to
2019                   notify the Master that it is ready, or else the Master may initiate the retry.
2020             ii. If Slave is not known to have a long wake period and to always monitor the I3C Bus,
2021                   then the Master marks the Slave as offline. The Master may then either retain the Slave’s
2022                   Dynamic Address for re-use, or else discard it.
2023                 The next action will be for the Slave to issue a Hot-Join request, in order to be
2024                 re-joined to the I3C Bus. In the Hot-Join operation the Master will assign the
2025                 Slave a Dynamic Address; the new Address may be either the same Address that
2026                 the Slave used before being marked as offline, or a different Address.




                                        Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                     93
                                                   All rights reserved.
                                                      Confidential
Specification for I3C Basic                                               Version 1.0
                                                                          19-Jul-2018

                                         All rights reserved.

       5.2       High Data Rate (HDR) Modes
2027   I3C Basic does not support the HDR Modes specified in the I3C Specification version 1.x [MIPI02], but per
2028   Table 3, I3C Basic Devices must detect the HDR Enter, HDR Exit, and HDR Restart patterns in order to be
2029   compatible with I3C v1.x Devices. I3C v1.x’s HDR Modes are designed to transfer more data at the same
2030   Bus frequency.
2031   Note:
2032         It is important to note that the I3C Bus is always initialized and configured in SDR Mode, never in any
2033         of the HDR Modes.
2034   I3C v1.x’s HDR Modes have Bus-wide effect. That is, the whole I3C Bus can be put into a given HDR Mode,
2035   and once entered that HDR Mode shall remain in effect until the end of that transaction.
2036   An HDR Mode period on the I3C Bus involves five steps:
2037   1. The I3C v1.x Master Broadcasts an Enter HDR Mode CCC, indicating which particular HDR
2038      Mode to enter. (See the Command Codes Enter HDR Mode 1 through Enter HDR Mode 8
2039      [ENTHDR1–ENTHDR8] in Section 5.1.9.3.9).
2040   2. The I3C v1.x Devices on the Bus switch to the requested HDR Mode.
2041   3. The I3C v1.x Master issues an HDR Command, followed by HDR data sent by the I3C v1.x
2042      Master or I3C v1.x Slave Device.
2043   4. An HDR Restart Pattern or HDR Exit Pattern (see Section 5.2.1) is sent.
2044       • If an HDR Restart Pattern, then a new HDR Command is sent.
2045   5. An I3C STOP, which ends with the Bus Free Condition.

       5.2.1        HDR Exit Pattern and HDR Restart Pattern
2046   Once an HDR Mode is entered, the HDR Exit Pattern is used to leave it, always exiting back to SDR Mode.
2047   The same HDR Exit Pattern is used to exit all HDR Protocols; that Pattern does not appear in any HDR
2048   Protocol’s normal data or command flow. All I3C Slaves shall detect and respond to the HDR Exit Pattern,
2049   irrespective of whether the Slave supports any particular HDR Mode. The HDR Exit Pattern Detector is
2050   specified in Section 5.2.1.3.
2051   As an alternative to the HDR Exit Pattern, the HDR Restart Pattern is also available. The HDR Restart Pattern
2052   allows multiple Messages to be sent while in HDR Mode, without forcing intervening exits to SDR Mode.
2053   That is: While the I3C Bus is in a given HDR Mode, an HDR Command can be sent to or from a Slave, and
2054   then the HDR Restart Pattern can be used to immediately send another HDR Command to or from the Slave
2055   (or a different Slave), without needing to exit the current HDR Mode between the HDR Commands. All I3C
2056   Slaves shall detect and respond to the HDR Restart Pattern while operating in any HDR Mode that the Slave
2057   supports. The HDR Restart Pattern Detector is specified in Section 5.2.1.4. Note that unlike the HDR Exit
2058   Pattern, the HDR Restart Pattern is only detected by I3C Slaves that support the current HDR Mode.




                                         Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                     95
                                                    All rights reserved.
                                                       Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                   Version 1.0
                                                                                                    19-Jul-2018

       5.2.1.1        HDR Exit Pattern
2059   The HDR Exit Pattern is defined thus:
2060    • SDA starts High, SCL starts Low
2061    • SDA falls (from High to Low) 4 times, while SCL remains Low (for the whole time)
2062    • Each SDA transition is separated by a time interval of at least tDIG_H (see Section 6.2)
2063    • At the end of the HDR Exit Pattern, first SCL rises and then SDA rises. This is a normal I3C STOP.
2064   Figure 25 illustrates an HDR Exit Pattern in the upper diagram (with edges to set SCL and SDA up into
2065   correct state), and an HDR Exit Pattern with appended I3C STOP in the lower diagram (SCL being High
2066   while SDA rises).

                                                   HDR Exit Pattern


                        SDA




                         SCL




                                               HDR Exit Pattern Plus STOP
                      Setup (SDA/SCL)                                            End
                           (If Needed)                                           Exit      STOP




                     SDA




2067                 SCL
2068                               Figure 25 HDR Exit Pattern and Exit Plus STOP




       96                                Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                    All rights reserved.
                                                       Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                     Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

       5.2.1.2        HDR Restart Pattern
2069   The HDR Restart Pattern is based on a subset of the HDR Exit Pattern. It is defined thus:
2070    • SDA starts High, SCL starts Low (same as HDR Exit Pattern)
2071    • SDA toggles 4 times (fall, rise, fall, rise)
2072    • The next edge is SCL rising. SDA may change with SCL rising, but SCL shall rise.
2073   Figure 26 illustrates an HDR Restart Pattern (with edges to set SCL and SDA up into correct state) along
2074   with the necessary SCL ending edge.

                                              HDR Restart Pattern
                                Setup (SDA/SCL)                     Possible
                                    (If Needed)                      Restart




                             SDA
                                                  2     2       2       2



                              SCL                                                   SCL
                                                                                    Edge
                                                                                    Validates
2075
2076                                  Figure 26 HDR Restart with Next Edge




                                       Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                               97
                                                  All rights reserved.
                                                     Confidential

                                                                                                       19-Jul-2018

       5.2.1.3          HDR Exit Pattern Detector
2077   All I3C Slaves shall include an HDR Exit Pattern Detector. The HDR Exit Pattern Detector shall be enabled
2078   only after an HDR Mode is entered, and shall be disabled after an HDR Exit pattern is detected. The HDR
2079   Exit Pattern Detector may be implemented either in digital logic, or in software (bit banged).
2080   The remainder of this Section presents an example digital logic implementation in both schematic view and
2081   in RTL code.
2082   The basic logic model is that SCL is held Low (0), and so SCL High (1) shall reset the detector. It also only
2083   uses falling edges of SDA, hence treats SDA as a clock. The HDR Exit Pattern Detector schematic is shown
2084   in Figure 27.

                    1    D       Q             D       Q               D       Q              D        Q       STOP


                 ~SDA            R      ~SDA           R      ~SDA             R      ~SDA             R



2085   ~SCL & isHDR

2086                         Figure 27 Example HDR Exit Pattern Detector (Schematic)

2087   The Detector uses an inverted version of SDA as a clock (so positive edge logic, but can use negative edge
2088   logic), and is reset when SCL is High (1), or when the block is not in HDR Mode. The asynchronous nature
2089   of the reset makes this safe. This is shown in Figure 28. Due to the nature of SCL vs. SDA changing at the
2090   same time with HDR Modes, the Bus Slave may see SCL change after on SDA and before the next. If the
2091   HDR Exit Pattern Detector were only using clocking logic, then it would not see any change at all (SDA
2092   posedge would always see SCL Low in this example). Because the Detector uses an asynchronous reset on
2093   SCL, a change in SCL will impact the counter, even in case (b) above. Note that SCL and SDA will still be
2094   approximately 50ns between changes. So, as shown, if SCL rises at any time, then the HDR Exit Pattern
2095   Detector shall be reset, and therefore will not mistakenly signal a false Exit.


                                 SDA                             SDA


                                  SCL                             SCL

                                a) As Emitted by HDR                 b) As Seen by Slave
2096
2097        Figure 28 Metastable Changes on SCL and SDA Do Not Break the Exit Pattern Detector

2098   An example RTL implementation of the above schematic is shown in Listing 1.

                                                   All rights reserved.
                                                      Confidential
       Version 1.0                                                                       Specification for I3C Basic
       19-Jul-2018

2099                         Listing 1 Example RTL Code for HDR Exit Pattern Generator
2100     wire                  scl_rst_n;
2101     wire                  SDA_clk_n;            // ~SDA as clock
2102     reg[3:0]             stp_cnt;              // HDR STOP counter (shift chain)
2103
2104     assign scl_rst_n = ~iSCL & iIsInHDR; // SCL=1 resets
2105     // next uses glitch free XOR for SDA clock. Only inverts
2106     // SDA as clock if not in scan. Could add a clock mux
2107     // so uses one common clock in scan.
2108     SAFE_CLK_XOR sda_neg_clk(iSDA, ~scan_mode, SDA_clk_n);
2109
2110     // counter for 3 and 4 rising SDA when SCL is High
2111     // (else SCL resets). Whole thing held in reset if
2112     // not in HDR Mode
2113     always @ (posedge SDA_clk_n or negedge scl_rst_n)
2114       if (~scl_rst_n)
2115         stp_cnt <= 4'd0;          // SCL High or not HDR
2116       else
2117         stp_cnt <= {stp_cnt[2:0], 1'b1}; // shift chain counter
2118
2119     assign oHDR_STOP = stp_cnt[3]; // detects Exit (STOP)

       5.2.1.4             HDR Restart and Exit Pattern Detector
2120   Any I3C Slave that supports at least one HDR Mode shall include HDR Restart Pattern detection. While this
2121   function is easily incorporated into the required HDR Exit Pattern Detector, or may be part of HDR Mode
2122   support, the particular design is not mandated by this Specification (i.e. is up to the manufacturer). The HDR
2123   Restart Pattern Detector may be implemented either in digital logic or in software (bit banged).
2124   The remainder of this Section presents an example digital logic implementation in both schematic view and
2125   in RTL code, building upon the HDR Exit Pattern Detector presented in Section 5.2.1.3.
2126   The basic logic model is that SCL is held Low (0) and so SCL High (1) will reset the main detector. It also
2127   uses falling edges of SDA primarily, hence treats SDA as a clock. The HDR Restart is then detected only
2128   when two falling edges have been seen, and verifies the rising edge and then SCL change that is required for
2129   an HDR Restart.
2130   The combined HDR Restart and Exit Pattern Detector schematic is shown in Figure 29.
                                                                  D      Q                       D         Q        HDR
                                                                                                                    Restart
                                                                              isHDR
                                                           SDA           R                SCL              R


                                                                                            isHDR & ~restart_done




                 1     D       Q              D      Q            D      Q                       D         Q        HDR
                                                                                                                    Stop

             ~SDA              R    ~SDA             R    ~SDA           R               ~SDA              R



       ~SCL & isHDR
2131
2132                  Figure 29 Combined HDR Restart and Exit Pattern Detector (Schematic)

2133   This Detector design builds on the HDR Exit Pattern Detector. After exactly two SDA falling edges are seen,
2134   followed by a rising edge, the HDR Restart Pattern Detector checks for the rising edge of SCL. It does not
2135   matter whether SDA also falls at the same time; the rising SCL is the key. This works because even if SDA


                                           Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                  99
                                                      All rights reserved.
                                                         Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                          Version 1.0
                                                                                                            19-Jul-2018

2136   were falling (and therefore triggering the next flop in the Exit Pattern Detector), the upper-left flop will still
2137   hold 1 because it has not yet seen a rising edge on SDA.
2138   An example RTL implementation of the above schematic is shown in Listing 2.
2139         Listing 2 Example RTL Code for Combined HDR Pattern Detector: Exit and Reset
2140     wire                scl_rst_n;
2141     wire                restart_rst_n;
2142     wire                SDA_clk_n;      // ~SDA as clock
2143     reg[3:0]           stp_cnt;        // HDR STOP counter (shift chain)
2144     reg                 poss_restart;   // possible restart
2145     reg                 is_restart;
2146
2147     assign scl_rst_n     = ~iSCL & iIsInHDR; // SCL=1 resets
2148     assign restart_rst_n = ~iRestartDone & iIsInHDR;
2149
2150     // next uses glitch free XOR for SDA clock. Only inverts
2151     // SDA as clock if not in scan. Could add a clock mux
2152     // so uses one common clock in scan.
2153     SAFE_CLK_XOR sda_neg_clk(iSDA, ~scan_mode, SDA_clk_n);
2154
2155     // counter for 3 and 4 rising SDA when SCL is High
2156     // (else SCL resets). Whole thing held in reset if
2157     // not in HDR Mode
2158     always @ (posedge SDA_clk_n or negedge scl_rst_n)
2159       if (~scl_rst_n)
2160         stp_cnt <= 4'd0;          // SCL High or not HDR
2161       else
2162         stp_cnt <= {stp_cnt[2:0], 1'b1}; // shift chain counter
2163
2164     assign oHDR_STOP = stp_cnt[3];// detects Exit (STOP)
2165
2166     // Possible Restart means exactly 2 falling edges
2167     // of SDA and then rising edge of SDA. Actual
2168     // Restart is from SCL then rising
2169     always @ (posedge iSDA or negedge restart_rst_n)
2170       if (~restart_rst_n)
2171         poss_restart <= 1'b0;     // Restart ACK or not HDR
2172       else if (stp_cnt[1] & ~stp_cnt[2]) // after 2nd fall only
2173         poss_restart <= 1’b1;     // SDA rise after 2 falls
2174       else
2175         poss_restart <= 1’b0;     // else not possible restart
2176
2177     always @ (posedge SCL or negedge restart_rst_n)
2178       if (~restart_rst_n)
2179         is_restart <= 1'b0;       // Restart ACK or not HDR
2180       else if (poss_restart)
2181         is_restart <= 1’b1;       // SCL rises after SDA does
2182       else
2183         is_restart <= 1’b0;       // else not restart

       5.2.2       HDR Double Data Rate Mode (HDR-DDR)
2186   This section is not included in the I3C Basic Specification. To gain access to this capability, please contact
2187   MIPI Alliance.
2188   Like SDR Mode, HDR-DDR Mode uses SCL as a clock; however unlike SDR, Data and Commands change
2189   SDA on both SCL edges (when High and when Low), effectively doubling the data rate. By contrast, in SDR
2190   Mode SDA is changed only when SCL is Low. Since SDR Mode defines it as a START or a STOP for SDA
2191   to change while SCL remains High, HDR-DDR Mode is classified as an HDR Mode in order to prevent
2192   confusion.

       5.2.2.1         HDR-DDR Overview
2193   This section is not included in the I3C Basic Specification. To gain access to this capability, please contact
2194   MIPI Alliance.

       5.2.2.2         HDR-DDR Command Coding

2196   MIPI Alliance.

2198   MIPI Alliance.

       5.2.3       HDR Ternary Modes (HDR-TSP and HDR-TSL)
2209   This section is not included in the I3C Basic Specification. To gain access to this capability, please contact
2210   MIPI Alliance.
2211   I3C defines two HDR Modes that use Ternary Coding:
2212     • HDR-TSP: Ternary Symbol for Pure Bus (no I2C Devices)
2213     • HDR-TSL: Ternary Symbol Legacy-inclusive-Bus
2214   These HDR Ternary Modes are entered in the standard way (see Section 5.1.9.3.9), followed by a Command
2215   and then zero or more Data Words. In HDR Ternary Modes, Commands shall be issued only by a Master.
2216   Data Words may be issued by a Master or by a Slave, depending on the particular Command (Write or Read).

       5.2.3.1         HDR Ternary Signaling and Coding Protocol
2217   This section is not included in the I3C Basic Specification. To gain access to this capability, please contact
2218   MIPI Alliance.

       6     I3C Electrical Specifications

       6.1      DC I/O Characteristics
2225   This Section describes the DC operating parameters of the I3C interface in two modes: Push-Pull Mode and
2226   Open Drain Mode. In Push-Pull Mode, the SDA pin drives at higher speeds with a totem-pole driver.
2227   Two important parameters should be noted for I3C:
2228    • The I3C interface targets nominal operating voltages of 1.2V, 1.8V, and 3.3V or less. The I3C
2229      interface is not characterized for 5V systems, but could be extended to support 5V if sufficient
2230      driver strength, reduced diameter, and/or reduced speed is used in the system.
2231    • For peak speeds the capacitance loading allowed is reduced to 50 pF, which is much lower than
2232      Legacy I2C. With I3C higher capacitance busses are possible, but only at reduced speeds and with
2233      reduced features (e.g. no I2C Devices are supported).
2234   Optionally, the I3C Basic interface also targets a nominal operating voltage of 1.0V and a 100 pF capacitive
2235   loading for new usages, such as Serial Presence Detect (SPD) in DDR5.




                                      Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.                                103
                                                 All rights reserved.
                                                    Confidential
       Specification for I3C Basic                                                                                                                             Version 1.0
                                                                                                                                                               19-Jul-2018

2239   I3C supports Legacy I2C Slaves. An important feature of an I2C Slave is the 50 ns Spike Filter on the SDA
2240   and SCL pads. If a Spike Filter is implemented on all I2C Devices present on the I3C Bus, then the I3C Bus
2241   may operate at maximum rated clock frequency. If any I2C Device does not have a Spike Filter, then the I3C
2242   Bus speed is determined by the slowest Legacy I2C Device without a Spike Filter. Other requirements of an
2243   I2C Legacy Slave are listed in Table 56.
2244                   Table 56 Legacy I2C Device Requirements When Operating on I3C

       Fm+ Speed                        –                X                 –                –               –
       HS and UFm                       –                –                 X                –               2
       Static I2C Address              X                 –                 –                –               –
       50 ns Spike Filter               –                X                 –                –               1
       Clock Stretching
                                        –                –                 –                X               –
       by Slave
       I2C Extended
                                        –                –                 X                –               2
       Address (10 bit)
       I3C Reserved
                                        –                –                 –                X               –
       Address
       Note:
         1)    Lack of Spike Filter will severely degrade Bus performance and eliminate certain I3C Bus features
         2)    “Not Used” means that the I3C Master will not make use of the I2C feature, however if the Slave
               supports the feature, then it will not interfere with I3C Bus operation.

       6.2     Timing Specification
2245   A key feature of I3C is to maximize the speed of data transfer and minimize the time required for low-power
2246   Devices to remain in Active Mode. In Single Data Rate Mode this is accomplished by keeping Bus
2247   capacitance low and clock speed high. For large packets of data an additional speed improvement is possible
2248   using HDR Modes, where data is transferred on every clock edge. I3C supports Legacy I2C Fm and Fm+
2249   Modes. Table 57 gives reference timing requirements used in Legacy Mode.

       112                                                            Copyright © 2016-2018 MIPI Alliance, Inc.
                                                                                 All rights reserved.
                                                                                    Confidential
Version 1.0                                                                                                                                                     Specification for I3C Basic
19-Jul-2018

     Note:
     1) FSCL = 1 / (tDIG_L + tDIG_H)
     2) tDIG_L and tDIG_H are the clock Low and High periods as seen at the receiver end of the I3C Bus using VIL and VIH (see Figure 30)
     3) When communicating with an I3C Device on a mixed Bus, the tDIG_H_MIXED period must be constrained in order to make sure that I2C Devices do not interpret I3C
         signaling as valid I2C signaling.
     4) As both edges are used, the hold time needs to be satisfied for the respective edges; i.e., tCF + 3 for falling edge clocks, and tCR + 3 for rising edge clocks.
     5) The clock maximum rise/fall time is capped at 60 ns. For lower frequency rise and fall the maximum value is limited at 60 ns, and is not dependent upon the clock frequency.
     6) tHD_PP is a Hold time parameter for Push-Pull Mode that has a different value for Master mode vs. Slave mode. In SDR Mode the Hold time parameter is referred to as tHD_SDR.
      7) Devices with more than 12ns of tSCO delay shall set the limitation bit in the BCR, and shall support the GETMXDS CCC to allow the Master to read this value and adjust
          computations accordingly. For purposes of system design and test conformance, this parameter should be considered together with pad delay, bus capacitance, propagation
          delay, and clock triggering points.
     8) Pad delay based on 90 Ω / 4 mA driver and 50 pF load. Note that Master may be a Slave in a multi-Master system, and thus shall also adhere to this requirement

2260                                                 Figure 31 tDIG_H and tDIG_L
2261   The start of a typical I3C communication in SDR Mode is shown in Figure 32. The initial communication
2262   looks very similar to I2C, with additional commands that follow as described in Section 4. The key difference
2263   is that higher clock speed is supported, up to 12.5 MHz. The higher clock speed allows Legacy I2C Devices
2264   with 50 ns Spike Filters to ignore the communications. The Master can then run in SDR Mode for I3C
2265   Devices using full 12.5 MHz timing, though it will have to slow down in order to communicate with Legacy
2266   I2C Devices. Figure 32 shows the beginning of a transaction, where the Slave acknowledges its address.
2267   Figure 33 shows the possible continuation of an I3C SDR communication, in the case where the Slave does
2268   not acknowledge its address. The Master may either STOP the communication, or else continue with a
2269   Repeated START.

       Annex D Typical I3C Basic Protocol Communications
       Figure 64 through Figure 66 each illustrate a typical communication for the I3C SDR Protocol. While these
       diagrams do not exhaustively illustrate all possible I3C SDR communications, they do serve as useful
       introductions to the signaling and transmission formatting used in the I3C SDR Protocol.
       Figure 64 illustrates example communication using I3C Single Data Rate (SDR) coding. It shows the Master
       reading a byte of data from the Slave at Address 0x2B in SDR Mode.
       From the Bus Free Condition, the Master issues a START by driving the SDA line Low while keeping the
       SCL line High. It then issues the Broadcast Address (7’h7E) followed by RnW (0 for Write). Then the Master
       turns on a pull-up resistor and goes to Open Drain. All Slaves ACK by pulling the SDA line Low (in the
       Figure, pink fill means the Slave is in control of the SDA line at this time). The Master then issues a Repeated
       START, then the Address of the Slave (0x2B) it wants to read followed by RnW (1 for Read). The Master
       then turns on a pull-up resistor and goes to Open Drain, allowing the Slave to acknowledge by pulling the
       SDA line Low. At this point, the Master continues to toggle the SCL line and release the SDA line, allowing
       the Slave to drive SDA to send one byte of data (0x4A) followed by ‘T’. T=1 informs the Master that there
       is additional data, whereas T=0 signals the end. Here there is additional data, so the Slave drives SDA High
       until SCL goes High, at which time it releases SDA. The Master has the option of holding SDA High with a
       weak pull-up, which signals to the Slave that the Master allows another byte to be transmitted, or to pull SDA
       Low (while SCL is High – hence a Repeated START), which would signal to the Slave that the Master has
       terminated the Read and is taking over.
       SDR Mode is backwards compatible with Legacy I2C Devices, because the High time of an SCL pulse is
       always less than 50ns and therefore SCL will always appear to be Low because of the I2C 50ns Spike Filter.


                     I3C SDR                                              SDR Message 1

                START    Broadcast        Sr             Addr                  Data…                      Data…,                 STOP

                                                    2B + Read                     4A   Note:
                                                                                       Slight dip on SDA indicates weak Pull-Up. Master may
                                                                                       choose to terminate Read and take over.

                                                                           0x4A    = 0b01001010                                               T

       Figure 65 shows the Master issuing a CCC Direct Command to a single Slave. This particular command
       (GETPID) reads the Provisional ID of a Slave.
       From the Bus Free Condition, the Master issues a START by driving the SDA line Low while keeping the
       SCL line High. It then issues the Broadcast Address (7’h7E) followed by RnW (0 for Write). Then the Master
       turns on a pull-up resistor and goes to Open Drain. All Slaves ACK by pulling SDA Low (in the Figure, pink
       fill means the Slaves are in control of SDA at this time). The Master then issues the Direct Common
       Command Code for GETPID (0x8C) followed by parity bit ‘T’ (odd parity = 0 for 0x8C) then the 7-bit
       Dynamic Address of the Slave (chosen arbitrarily here to be 0x2B) followed by a RnW bit (1 for Read). Then
       the Master turns on a pull-up resistor and goes to Open Drain, allowing the Slave at Address 0x2B to ACK
       by pulling SDA Low, which tells the Master that the Slave Acknowledges the command and will comply.
       (Alternatively, the Slave may NACK by not pulling SDA Low, which would inform the Master that the Slave
       will not comply – in this case, that an error occurred.) Following the ACK the Slave outputs its 48-bit PID
       one byte at a time, and then the Master issues a Repeated START (this part of the waveform sequence is not
       shown in the Figure).

               START    Broadcast      SDR Cmd            Sr      Address                       Reply Data…,          Sr

       Figure 66 illustrates example SDR communication with a CCC Broadcast command. The command used in
       this example sets the Maximum Read Length of all Slaves to 43 bytes (0x002B).
       From the Bus Free Condition, the Master issues a START by driving the SDA line Low while keeping the
       SCL line High. It then issues the Broadcast Address (7’h7E) followed by RnW (0 for Write). Then the Master
       turns on a pull-up resistor and goes to Open Drain. All Slaves ACK by pulling SDA Low (in the Figure, pink
       fill means the Slaves are in control of SDA at this time). The Master then issues the Broadcast Common
       Command Code for SETMRL (0x09) followed by parity bit ’T’ (odd parity = 1 for 0x09), and then 2 data
       bytes (MSb first) to define the maximum number of bytes which can be read from a Slave in a single read
       operation. Each data byte is followed by a ‘T’ bit (parity bit – odd parity). After this the Master issues a
       Repeated START.

                  I3C SDR                                                    SDR Message 1

            START      Broadcast      SDR_Cmd                   Data…, MSB                   Data…,LSB                Sr

                                      Broadcast                       00                        2B
                                    CCC_SETMRL
                                                      Hex      Binary   T
                                                      0x00 = 0b00000000 1
                                                          (this segment not shown)

                                                    0x2B      = 0b00101011                                     T    Sr

               Licensing Terms
2358   This agreement (the “Agreement”) between MIPI Alliance Inc. (“MIPI”) and each MIPI member or other
2359   party that has manifest agreement to these terms (each a “Licensor” and collectively the “Licensors”) is
2360   effective as of the date the I3C Basic Specification (defined below) is first approved by the MIPI Board (the
2361   “Effective Date”). Capitalized terms used in this Agreement that are not expressly defined here have the
2362   meaning identified in the MIPI Membership Agreement or MIPI Bylaws, as applicable. For convenience,
2363   key definitions are reproduced in Attachment A. For the avoidance of doubt: (a) in connection with this
2364   Agreement, any reference to a “MIPI Specification” means the MIPI I3C Basic Specification as described in
2365   Section 2, and (b) any rights or obligations created under this Agreement are independent of any rights or
2366   obligations created under the MIPI Membership Agreement, Bylaws or any other agreements, and nothing in
2367   this Agreement is intended to alter rights or obligations established elsewhere.
2368   1.       Background. Typically, MIPI specifications are implemented only by MIPI members. MIPI
2369   members make certain intellectual property licensing commitments to other members under the MIPI
2370   Membership Agreement, with different rules applying to “Mobile Terminals” and “Accessories,” in contrast
2371   to other types of implementations. The MIPI Board intends to make the MIPI I3C Basic Specification
2372   available for implementation by parties who are non-members of MIPI, however. Further, both MIPI
2373   members and non-members may use this specification inside and outside of Mobile Terminals or Accessories.
2374   The Licensors contributed to the development of the MIPI I3C Basic Specification, and desire to see it widely
2375   used. MIPI and the Licensors believe that making licenses available (as set forth in this Agreement) to both
2376   member and non-member implementers, for all types of implementations, will facilitate widespread adoption
2377   of the MIPI I3C Basic Specification, to the benefit of MIPI, the Licensors, and the broader community that
2378   MIPI serves.
2379   2.        MIPI I3C Basic Specification. “MIPI I3C Basic Specification” means the specification titled “I3C
2380   Basic 1.0” as approved by the MIPI Board, and all subsequent versions of such specification approved by the
2381   MIPI Board after the Effective Date. Any party implementing the MIPI I3C Basic Specification, whether or
2382   not a MIPI member, is an “Implementer.” For the avoidance of doubt: the MIPI I3C Basic Specification is
2383   distinct from the MIPI I3C Specification version 1.0 approved by the MIPI Board on Dec. 31, 2016. The
2384   terms of this Agreement apply exclusively to the MIPI I3C Basic Specification.
2385   3.       License commitment.
2386        a. RAND-Z license obligation. For the MIPI I3C Basic Specification only, Licensor hereby
2387        agrees to grant, and to cause its Affiliates to grant, to any requesting Implementer a worldwide,
2388        non-exclusive, non-sublicensable license under the Necessary Claims of Licensor or its Affiliates,
2389        with zero royalties or other compensation, under terms and conditions that are reasonable and
2390        nondiscriminatory, to make, have made, use, import, offer to sell, lease, sell, promote and
2391        otherwise distribute Compliant Portions. Licensor shall not be obligated to license any part or
2392        function of a product in which a Compliant Portion is incorporated that is not itself a Compliant
2393        Portion.
2394        b. Reciprocity; defensive suspension. Licensor shall not be obligated to license any Implementer
2395        if that Implementer does not agree to make patent licenses available under any Necessary Claims
2396        of that Implementer and its Affiliates to Licensors and all other Implementers under terms
2397        substantially identical to the terms described in this Agreement. Further, a Licensor may suspend
2398        any license granted under this Agreement to any Implementer if that Implementer or its Affiliate
2399        initiates against any party litigation that alleges infringement of a Necessary Claim of Implementer
2400        or its Affiliate in connection with the MIPI I3C Basic Specification. Additionally, subject to
2401        Section 3.1(f) of the MIPI Membership Agreement as between MIPI Members, a Licensor may
2402        terminate, ab initio, any license granted pursuant to this Agreement to any Implementer that
2403        initiates litigation against the Licensor alleging infringement of any patent claim of the
2404        Implementer or its Affiliate. For the purposes of this Agreement, a party that files a suit which is

2407         c. Circumvention or transfer. Licensor agrees that it has not transferred and will not transfer any

2425   termination that are used in a substantially similar manner and to a substantially similar extent with a
2426   substantially similar result as the Necessary Claims that were used in a prior version for which the Licensor
2427   is obligated to grant licenses under this Agreement; and (c) for those Necessary Claims that directly result
2428   from inclusion of Licensor-provided material in the draft version of the specification that existed immediately
2429   prior to Licensor’s withdrawal. Termination of this Agreement by one Licensor does not impact the
2430   Agreement among MIPI or the other Licensors, not does it not impact Licensor’s MIPI Membership
2431   Agreement, which will remain in full force and effect.
2432   5.        Third party beneficiaries. All Implementers, whether or not they are MIPI members, are intended
2433   third party beneficiaries of this Agreement.

2435   counterparts. If additional parties manifest agreement to terms substantially identical to this Agreement, those
2436   parties will be deemed Licensors under this Agreement.
